#2904. Top Wall Street analysts are upbeat about the prospects for these 3 stocks

September ended on a mixed note, with the S&P 500 and Dow Jones Industrial Average falling 0.5% and 4.3%, respectively, while the Nasdaq Composite climbed 1.9%. Markets remained on edge with higher oil prices and Treasury yields weighing on investor sentiment and triggering fears of additional interest rate hikes.

In this climate, investors with a long-term horizon can track recommendations from top Wall Street analysts to find stocks with solid growth prospects.

Here are three stocks favored by some of Wall Street's top pros, according to TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Neocloud company CoreWeave (CRWV) is benefiting from robust AI-driven demand for cloud computing.

Recently, JPMorgan analyst Samik Chatterjee upgraded CoreWeave to buy from hold, raising his price target to $125 from $120. The analyst cited favorable pricing, driven by solid demand, for the upgrade.

Chatterjee said CoreWeave is now more willing to sign premium priced, shorter-term contracts, which lift his revenue and margin estimates. CRWV stock performance has been underwhelming as investors worry about high capital spending, the analyst noted. That said, he believes that higher prices and increased margins will more than offset the rise in debt to fund capital spending, improving the stock's valuation.

Chatterjee noted the 25% price hike in July across CoreWeave's product offerings and frequent price increases by rivals like Nebius (NBIS), adding that some peers are charging nearly three times more for short-term compute contracts than what CoreWeave charges on longer-term deals.

"CoreWeave's leverage of the opportunity [is] evident in its recent press release highlighting contracts signed in F3Q at ~$40M/MW," said Chatterjee. JPMorgan said higher pricing is already contributing 5-10 percentage points to contract margins versus earlier deals.

Chatterjee ranks No. 4 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 75% of the time, delivering an average return of 55.3%. See CoreWeave Statistics on TipRanks.

Palo Alto Networks (PANW) is a provider of cybersecurity solutions across network, cloud, security operations, AI and identity. Following a discussion with management, BTIG analyst Gray Powell reiterated a buy rating on PANW and raised his price target to $425 from $404.

After a review of Palo Alto's product-level growth catalysts, Powell sees clear upside to both revenue and next-generation security (NGS) annual recurring revenue (ARR) in fiscal 2027. He expects PANW to deliver pro forma revenue growth of more than 17%, above the consensus estimate of 14.6%, and NGS ARR growth of more than 26% versus the Street's expectation of 24.5%.

Over the past five years, the Palo Alto business mix, has moved to higher-growth product areas and new markets. from what was earlier viewed as a hardware-centric network security company, Powell said. The 5-star analyst estimates that hardware firewalls and attached subscriptions now account for less than 50% of revenue.

Meanwhile, high-growth solutions, including Prisma SASE, software firewalls, Cortex XSIAM, Chronosphere and Idira now represent more than 35% of PANW's revenue. "Investors may not fully realize the growth potential within PANW's platform story," Powell said.

BTIG is optimistic about the prospects of Chronosphere in observability, CyberArk in identity and software firewalls and Prisma AIRS in the network and AI security business.

Powell ranks No. 320 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 61% of the time, delivering an average return of 17.3%. See Palo Alto Networks KPIs on TipRanks.

In a research note on September 30, Rosenblatt analyst Scott Devitt reiterated a buy rating on e-commerce and cloud computing platform Amazon (AMZN), raising his price target to $360 from $335. Fears that agentic commerce poses a major threat to Amazon's advertising business seem "overstated," he said.

Devitt noted that the growing adoption of personal agents and AI shopping assistants has sparked concern about displacement of Amazon's retail media business. But the 5-star analyst remains bullish, given Amazon's ability to adapt to changing consumer behavior, as it's done in the past.

Additionally, Devitt argues that even if an AI agent helps a shopper discover a product, Amazon can still handle the purchase and delivery, keeping much of the transaction value. He highlighted that management believes that existing Amazon shoppers will likely continue to start their shopping journey on the platform because it has better personalization, accurate pricing and inventory information that third-party agents still lack.

Overall, Devitt expects Amazon's retail business to prove a net beneficiary of agentic commerce. Fewer clicks would not hurt the company if the value of each remaining click and overall volumes keep rising, he said. The argument is that even if Amazon loses some sponsored ad revenue over the next few years, it would likely be only a small part of the company's total business and could be offset by growth in other areas.

"Consistent with our AI framework, we continue to view Amazon as one of the most resilient leaders in our coverage, combining owned compute infrastructure with a physical fulfillment moat," said Devitt. His bullish view is supported, Devitt said, by expectations that Amazon Web Services (AWS) will exit the year at a more than 45% growth rate with continued margin improvement.

Devitt ranks No. 765 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 49% of the time, delivering an average return of 12%. See Amazon Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

#2901. ‘A dark day for Nvidia’: Here’s what analysts have been saying about Jensen Huang’s departure

Nvidia investors are preparing for a future without Jensen Huang after the CEO's resignation was announced on Thursday.

Markets were on tenterhooks ahead of the decision amid reports that the chip giant's board had convened to discuss his future with the company. By the time of the announcement, at least six analysts had downgraded the stock between Wednesday night and Thursday morning.

The New York Times on Wednesday reported that Huang had engaged in a second extramarital affair with an employee. The CEO was already under scrutiny after Huang admitted to having a romantic relationship with a woman U.S. intelligence believes is a Chinese spy.

Nvidia announced on Thursday morning that, following a board meeting, Huang would step down after more than 30 years at the company's helm.

CFO Colette Kress has been named interim CEO.

Those downgrading the stock included longtime Nvidia bull Ben Reitzes of Melius Research.

Nvidia shares were seen down more than 12% shortly after the open on Wall Street, bringing losses for the last month close to 25%.

Here's what analysts and investors are saying about the company and the stock.

Reitzes changed his rating for Nvidia to hold from buy. His price target is now $185 — down from $315 — is 6% above the level at which shares last closed.

"We thought we had experience, but nothing prepares you for what has happened at Nvidia over the last few weeks - and especially since Saturday. The only situation remotely close to this we can recall was HP in 2010. Waiting to see what that Board would do (and who they would hire) that time didn't work out. It was really cheap, too, at ~9x EPS right after its initial swoon. Nvidia is a much stronger, more important company, but its market moves far faster, and this story touches geopolitical flashpoints. In this unique crisis, we have decided (reluctantly) to step aside on Nvidia (now a Hold) after recommending it since we launched our AI platform coverage universe in July 2023," he said.

Analyst Mark Lipacis downgraded Nvidia to in line from outperform. He also lowered his price target on shares to $186 from $465, implying 7% upside from Wednesday's close.

"The Wall Street Journal reported Nvidia's Board was 'preparing to oust' its CEO after he had two extramarital affairs, including one with a suspected Chinese spy. The NY Times reported that Nvidia's Board hired law firm Wachtell Lipton to lead an investigation into the matter. While Nvidia's Board said that it 'has no reason to believe that either national security or Nvidia secrets were compromised,' we believe that the investigation will take time and we expect NVDA's P/E multiple will continue to compress consistent with our 'Late-Innings of the Semi Cycle' thesis, especially since Nvidia has no obvious succession plan or number 2 operational executive," he said.

William Blair's Sebastien Naji downgraded Nvidia to market perform from outperform.

"After a head-spinning week of news and allegations around CEO Jensen Huang, Nvidia's board is reportedly preparing to remove him, according to the Wall Street Journal. The full story will take time to emerge and may require several investigations, including the board's own independent probe and a requested U.S. House briefing. What is already clear is that Huang's likely departure and heightened scrutiny of the company will be a significant overhang on NVDA shares in the near term. Meanwhile, the potential for a slower AI buildout and more intense competition from other chipmakers will only add to execution risk...governance and regulatory pressure, the potential for more serious fallout, and a sudden change in management are likely to muddle both execution and sentiment. As a result, we are downgrading NVDA to Market Perform and will wait for the dust to settle before reassessing," he said.

Analyst Rick Schafer downgraded the AI stock to perform from outperform.

"We are downgrading shares of NVDA and removing our price target following reports (WSJ, NYT) of founding CEO Jensen Huang's imminent departure amidst allegations of a 2nd extramarital affair. Credible media reports circulated Wednesday evening that NVDA's board is preparing to part ways with Huang following a widely reported relationship with an alleged Chinese spy compounded by new allegations of a relationship with an NVDA executive. This 'one-two' punch appears to have forced the board's hand...While we see upside to the F3Q (Oct.) print and F4Q (Jan.) outlook, we expect fundamentals to take a backseat near term. The departure of Mr. Huang could, in our view, translate to a lingering share overhang," he said.

Analyst Stacy Rasgon slashed his price target from $400 and downgraded the stock from market outperform.

"So now what? On the positive, the fundamentals of the business (if not the leadership) areclearly still intact with numbers that remain massive (and probably still headed higher). We don't think the company should have huge issues operating while engaged in a search for new leadership. And (of course) the shares are almost absurdly cheap (and getting cheaper as we speak); clearly there is a lot of bad news getting priced in here," he said.

"On the other hand, we find it hard to advocate stepping in here in size as there will no doubt be more allegations to come (there is a trope that says if you find two ants in your kitchen the best estimate of the total number of ants in your kitchen is not two...), both from internal issues as well as headlines related to the business, new governmental investigations etc. Adding on the new variable of who will take the reins (internal? external?) and anything else they may or may not uncover creates additional uncertainty (and we can't even begin to speculate as to who they might find to fill Jensen's shoes)," he added.

Analyst John Vinh downgraded the stock from overweight.

"We believe Huang's departure represents a material increase in strategic, organizational, and execution risk that warrants a higher risk premium and lower valuation multiple for the stock. While we expect core fundamentals to remain strong for the foreseeable future, we believe it will be challenging for NVDA to find a successor with the same leadership and strategic vision as Huang," he said.

"In our view the writing is on the wall that Jensen's days at Nvidia could be numbered and we would expect this to be a very fluid situation over the next 48 hours," renowned tech bull Dan Ives said in a note overnight.

"Jensen has created a black eye moment for himself and Nvidia and now has created a spider web of complexity that Wall Street, Silicon Valley, DC, and the global tech ecosystem are trying to figure out what is next."

"We believe Nvidia's value is $300 per share with Jensen as CEO and $225 per share without Jensen as CEO," Ives added.

"The stock will clearly be under pressure with this fluid news but the reality is its a dark day for Nvidia, but the company will navigate this turmoil and get through to the other side and remain a core foundational piece of the AI Revolution."

Citi analysts said overnight that they had trimmed their target price for Nvidia to $225, down from $285.

"If Jensen were forced out, it would mark the end of an era," Malik and Sylla said in their note.

"Under his leadership, Nvidia became the dominant force in AI infrastructure, the primary beneficiary of the global AI spending cycle, and one of the world's most valuable companies. A leadership transition creates an overhang on the stock given his central role in strategy, product vision, customer relationships, and investor confidence."

They said Nvidia CFO Colette Kress could serve as interim CEO while the board evaluates external candidates.

"As much as there is a lesson here for Nvidia around corporate governance, around key man risk… the reality is there is still 40,000 other employees at Nvidia, and there is some real talent there, and I have no doubt that there are some executors within Nvidia that the board will be able to tap to execute on their existing plans and continue to manage the company," Nathan Thooft, the firm's CIO, told CNBC's "Squawk Box Asia" on Thursday.

"I think the bigger risk is the foresight in the next several years, where Nvidia was driven by a lot of the mindset and thoughts that come out of their CEO, and if he's no longer there they're going to have to find that replacement."

"You can't deny the board has done a horrible job of succession planning," Greifeld told CNBC's "Morning Call" on Thursday.

"What the board has to find out in the next days or weeks is, did Jensen lie to them? Right? Did he lie to them repeatedly on different topics? We don't know. Does he lose their trust basically on lying to them over the years? I'm sure the board would love to keep Jensen in place in some role. Right, his value to the company is quite large."

Greifeld said he does not see a premium in Nvidia stock, noting that it trades at a low multiple "so there's no bubble to burst here with respect to the valuation."

"If there's a new CEO in, they don't have to make any decisions day one, day 30, day 60. They have time to really get their footing and come up with their own strategic vision," he told CNBC. "You're not going to change what is in the development plan for chips that exists today. So I think certainly it would be a loss, but given the nature of the business and the long lead time for these chip developments and the long contractual relationships they have with customers, it would give them certainly room to maneuver."

CIO Richard Reyle told CNBC's "Squawk Box Asia" that Nvidia's board had painted themselves into a corner by not having any kind of succession plan in place.

"I've heard him compared to Steve Jobs being removed from Apple, but [Jobs] put in place a succession plan and cultivated and groomed Tim Cook for years, so that when he passed away and [Cook] took over, there was a real leader there," he said. "Right now, Nvidia doesn't have that person in place, so it's going to be a choice of keeping Jensen and being faced with … all kinds of interrogations, or they're going to have to move on from him, and that's just going to make the stock uninvestable. That doesn't make the AI trade uninvestable, but it certainly makes Nvidia uninvestable."

Reyle said he saw the stock moving "lower and much lower from here."

"There's no obvious [replacement] there that can step right in and take over, so they may kind of try to ride this out and retain him," he said. "But there's going to be a lot of difficulty in doing so because he's going to become something of a liability."

"NVIDIA upheaval puts tech concentration risks in focus," Mark Haefele, global wealth management chief investment officer at UBS, and his team said on Thursday morning.

"Without taking any views on individual companies, the episode reinforces the importance of investment diversification and selectivity as the AI trade matures. We retain our conviction in AI's long-term potential, with compute demand remaining robust and earnings resilient. But elevated expectations and potentially wide differences in company outcomes can leave concentrated portfolios exposed to company-specific setbacks."

"Jensen Huang is toast," Bill Blain, founder and principal, said in his morning newsletter. "Nvidia's dependence on Jensen Huang is now exposed as a major market risk. The dominant firm within the AI industry lacks clear succession planning and Huang was seen as the acceptable face of AI by the public."

"Confidence in the AI sector has been wobbling for months," he added. "Huang's departure could lead to instability and trigger another collapse."

#2900. Warriors reveal Stephen Curry injury diagnosis after MRI on abdomen

The Golden State Warriors were already without one star. Now they'll be without their Most Valuable Player candidate, Stephen Curry, for the short-term future, too.

The Warriors announced Thursday, Nov. 12 that an MRI confirmed Curry suffered an oblique abdominal tear and added that he would be reevaluated in two weeks. The injury took place Wednesday, Nov. 11 in an eventual 115-108 loss against the Houston Rockets.

Curry walked off the floor midway through the first quarter, clearly grimacing and favoring his right side.

The sequence took place just 5:35 into the game. Curry had scored 3 points on 1-of-3 shooting by that point. He did not return to the floor for the rest of the first quarter, and he was not back at the bench area to start the second period.

ANALYSIS: What does Curry injury mean for Warriors? Can they weather the storm?

The Warriors announced minutes into the second quarter that Curry would not return for the rest of the game with right abdominal soreness.

After the game, Warriors coach Steve Kerr said that Curry said he "tweaked something in his abdominal area," which Curry told Kerr he had never done before. Kerr added that "concern is high" though he reiterated that he had no idea of the extent of the injury.

During the game, Curry did have a collision with Rockets forward Amen Thompson earlier in the game, when Curry had driven to the hoop on an attempted layup. Then, Curry had been fighting off a screen against Rockets center Alperen Şengün. As soon as that sequence ended, Curry once again grabbed his side.

Finally, just moments before he left the game, Curry curled around a screen and put up a 3-point shot. As soon as he landed and stepped backwards, he grabbed at his right side, near his hip.

Curry had been in the midst of a renaissance and had been elevating the play of the Warriors, who entered Wednesday night 8-3, which was tied for the third best record in the Western Conference.

Prior to suffering the injury, Curry was averaging 30.6 points, 4.1 rebounds and 4.3 assists per game in nine appearances. He was converting 3-pointers at a blistering 45.7%, which would mark a career-high. His scoring total entering Wednesday night had ranked second in the NBA, behind only Luka Dončić's historic 39.3 points per game, for all players with at least five games played.

The Warriors were already without Jimmy Butler, who has missed the start of the season as he rehabs the torn anterior cruciate ligament he suffered in January 2026.

#2899. Yankees President Randy Levine pushes college sports bill, discusses MLB labor negotiations

Sign up today for the CNBC Sport Newsletter

New York Yankees President Randy Levine is urging the House to pass the Protect College Sports Act, warning that college athletics could face deeper financial trouble without federal action. But critics of the bill say it will not do enough to protect student-athletes.

In an interview with CNBC's Michael Ozanian, Levine said the bill would create a $22 million pool for schools to retain their own players, along with an additional $5 million for women's and non-revenue sports. He said the House should act after the measure passed the Senate with 77 votes.

President Donald Trump appointed Levine as a vice-chair of a commission in February to explore college sports reforms. The Roundtable on Fixing College Sports was set up to bring more order to college sports as the rise of name, image and likeness deals and the transfer portal have dramatically changed the college sports landscape.

"If this bill isn't passed, there will never be a bill that's passed," Levine said, warning that the Big Ten and SEC could form a "super conference" that operates like a minor league.

Levine said features of the bill will also help level the playing field, provide health care and scholarship protection and help women's and non-revenue sports.

Opponents argue that the bill would deny college athletes certain rights and protections and would give more money to the NCAA, conferences, and schools to "consolidate money and power."

"The so-called Protect College Sports Act does little to actually protect the student-athletes who make this multi-billion-dollar industry possible," Derrick Johnson, president and CEO of the NAACP, said in a statement in September.

Levine also addressed the looming labor negotiations in Major League Baseball, where he serves on the league's labor committee.

The current collective bargaining agreement expires Dec. 1 and both sides appear to be preparing for the possibility of a work stoppage.

Asked whether there would be a 2027 season, he said, "I don't know whether they'll reach an agreement or not," citing "fundamental differences in opinion."

Levine said it's early and that these deals don't usually get done until the very end.

"I'm very confident that the sides will give it all they got to negotiate in good faith to get something done," he said.

#2898. Cleveland Browns defense starting to show some cracks in the armor

The Cleveland Browns defense has been feeling the pressure through the first nine weeks of the season.

Following the complete team meltdown in Week 1 against Jacksonville, the defense has carried more than its share of the water each week.

It has become a familiar script: the defense keeps the Browns in the game while the offense bumbles its way through the first three quarters, only to catch fire when it appears all hope is lost to pull out a win.

At least that is how it has played out with Deshaun Watson as the starting quarterback. Unfortunately for the Browns, Watson was sidelined for last week's game against the New Orleans Saints with a concussion, so no winning comeback was to be found.

While everyone knows that playing the way the Browns have through the first nine games is not sustainable, most weeks it felt that way given the way the defense was playing.

But the strain has started to show on the defense over the past few weeks; in the previous three games, they have allowed an average of 24.7 points and 167 rushing yards per game.

So what can the defense do to get itself right and help the Browns avoid heading into the bye week on a three-game losing streak?

When asked about it on Thursday, defensive coordinator Mike Rutenberg said it comes down to facing the challenge head-on and finding that extra bit of energy to make a difference:

"No one drive defines a game, and I think that's important for the whole team to know, and that's what Monk always talks about. Whether it's starting fast, finishing faster, making the play at the end of the half, going in, getting a three and out after halftime. Each little six-second play defines the game.

"And then of course at the end of the game, great defenses try their butts off to finish. And during the week, I'm not going to get into what we do, but what I can promise you is we are always addressing whatever came up head-on, honestly, together, and figuring out the best ways, whether it's in a meeting, whether it's out at practice, whether it's a drill. We do all that, and our guys are bought into that, and it's really cool to see."

But what about the run defense? While the Browns have never really been stout against the run, being ranked No. 27 against it (130.6 yards per game) this season doesn't make life any easier.

The Texas don't present a daunting challenge as they are ranked No. 29 in rushing (87.1 yards per game), but neither did the Saints, and they ripped off 141 rushing yards.

Rutenberg went back to his standby answer when asked about what the coaches can do this week to get things back on track:

"Every week, whatever it is, we address it, like I always say, because that's just what football is. And in terms of the run defense, great run games are based on violence, which is what we're built off of. Discipline fits and straining of the ball. And we'll continue to preach our style of strain and violence and our style of the mind to do our best in the fits, practice it like we've been doing it, and continue to, whatever it is, take that approach in everything we're trying to fix."

The joy of being 5-2 and first in the AFC North has started to slip away over the past two weeks, and the defense has played a role in that.

This week presents another challenge, and if the Browns hope to enter the bye with a victory, they are going to need the defense to get back to its particular strain of violence when the Texans have the ball.

#2896. Analysis: Nvidia’s leadership crisis tests the chipmaker’s role as the ‘central bank of AI’

Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang's shock resignation is raising a worrying question: Could trouble at the chipmaker spread through the tightly connected financial network Nvidia has built to support the artificial intelligence boom?

Follow CNBC's live coverage of Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang's resignation

Serious problems with Nvidia could do far-reaching economic and financial damage. The company has made hundreds of billions of dollars in financial commitments to other firms to help the industry grow, earning it the nickname the "central bank of AI." The Federal Reserve and other financial institutions are closely watching the AI buildout to monitor for the possibility that an unexpected event could destabilize the global financial system.

Even back in July Fed officials were discussing the possibility of a "broad-based repricing of assets" in the event of problems in the AI sector.

A rerun of the 2007-2008 financial crisis isn't likely, according to analysts and current and former financial officials. Nvidia isn't "too big to fail in a bank sense at all," said Sheila Bair, a former government regulator who ran the Federal Deposit Insurance Corp. Unlike a troubled bank, Nvidia isn't vulnerable to a sudden loss of confidence and would still produce a product customers want to buy even if something went wrong. And its massive equity gives it a cushion.

But regulators need to watch carefully for exposure by the banks and the wider financial system to Nvidia and other firms like it, Bair said.

"Nvidia going down would be a pretty seismic event," she said.

Investors still have high hopes for Nvidia, which reports earnings on Tuesday. Analysts expect its revenue to more than double in the current fiscal year, and then to roughly double again over the following two years, according to LSEG data. But Huang's exit from the company Thursday following a pair of extramarital affairs, including one with a woman The New York Times reported was suspected of spying for China, makes the company's fate extraordinarily hard to predict. Huang had led the company since co-founding it in 1993.

Nvidia didn't respond to a request for comment. The company named CFO Colette Kress as interim CEO.

Nvidia's stock fell 33% from its Oct. 6 peak through Thursday morning. Still, people who closely track the financial flows underlying AI say that even sharper losses by Nvidia likely wouldn't trigger the kind of systemic problems that regulators guard against.

And yet the risks flowing from AI are keeping some finance officials up at night. They are watching for the possibility that many of the factors that have helped the AI boom could reverse simultaneously. If the value of Nvidia's GPU chips fell, for instance, it could prompt investors to ask whether companies that borrowed money to buy them would be able to pay off their debts.

In that case, the financial assistance big tech firms have extended to others in the industry could suddenly become drags on their growth. Tech companies would have credit problems at the same time that their equity is falling. Problems among giant tech firms wouldn't stay contained to tech investors' portfolios, but could cause problems across the economy.

"I think that's a real concern because both the economy and financial markets are standing on the same leg, which means a drawdown could have reinforcing effects between the two," said Jason Wu, an assistant director at the International Monetary Fund who oversees the Global Markets Analysis unit.

Wu was the lead author of a chapter of the IMF's Global Financial Stability report published in October that warned about the potential for growing leverage among AI firms and investors to trigger broader problems. The so-called hyperscalers — the biggest tech firms — have grown from just 1% of bond issuance in the $15 trillion investment-grade debt market to 10% in just two years. Hedge funds and retail investors are making risky bets even riskier by taking on debt to buy stocks or investing in funds that have embedded leverage.

Nvidia is at the center of all of that. The company has repeatedly stepped in to help backstop smaller AI companies known as neoclouds, which build data centers using Nvidia chips and lease capacity to other firms. Among other obligations, Nvidia disclosed in July that it has committed to buy up to $36 billion in cloud services from those companies. That helps Nvidia ensure that there will be more customers for its AI chips, and it helps those companies find easier financing.

Wu and others refer to these kinds of arrangements as circular financing. When business is growing, it can be productive for bigger firms to help their customers buy their products. But if Nvidia couldn't honor its obligations, it would ripple through the firms that borrowed the money and the banks that lent to it. Relatively opaque private credit funds have also lent heavily into the AI boom, and how they would fare is a subject of debate among regulators.

Nvidia didn't reveal its specific partners, but some neoclouds such as CoreWeave and IREN have separately disclosed their relationships with the bigger firm.

Many neoclouds have been under stress in recent weeks. The cost of insuring CoreWeave debt, as measured by credit-default spreads, rose by 94% — nearly doubling — from Oct. 1 to Nov. 5, before settling, according to LSEG data. The price of an IREN convertible bond maturing in 2029 fell 23% from Nov. 3 to Nov. 5, amid a 26% decline in the company's share price.

Nvidia's other obligations include a $105 billion phased guarantee to a major data center project in Ohio planned by OpenAI and SB Energy, subject to certain conditions. Nvidia promised to backstop up to $125 billion of a $500 billion credit program by Wall Street financial firms.

These obligations come in different forms and are unlikely to come due all at once, if ever. Stijn Van Nieuwerburgh, a Columbia Business School finance professor, estimates them at $400 billion overall.

That is a huge sum, but still a small fraction of the roughly $3.9 trillion market capitalization Nvidia has even after its stock decline. "The company would have to get to the brink of extinction before all these guarantees come into jeopardy."

"I don't want to say it's a safe system, but I do want to say there's a lot of equity in the system right now," Van Nieuwerburgh said.

But in the past few days, Nvidia's troubles have separated it from the pack. Most neoclouds' equity prices have recovered this week as Nvidia's share price has fallen. That suggests investors see Nvidia's problems as specific to the company, for now.

Meanwhile the basic laws of economics are driving other firms to try to move into Nvidia's lucrative chips business. AMD, Broadcom, Intel, Google, Microsoft, and OpenAI all have efforts underway to build their own AI chips, Van Nieuwerburgh said.

Over time, they could replace Nvidia's products. That could cap the economic fallout of any potential troubles at the company.

And yet it's impossible to entirely write off the financial risks of the AI trade suddenly hitting a wall, added Van Nieuwerburgh, who spoke to me over Zoom from the sidelines of a bimonthly meeting of finance officials in Basel, Switzerland.

Central bankers "are clearly very, very, very anxious about this and thinking hard about these questions," he said.

#2894. Nvidia stock closes down over 4% after CEO Jensen Huang announces resignation

Follow CNBC's live coverage of Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang's resignation here.

Nvidia shares declined 4.34% Thursday after Jensen Huang announced he would immediately step down as CEO and a member of the board at the chipmaker.

The stock hovered under $4 trillion in market cap for most of the day, but climbed slightly heading into the close to finish above the number. The 4% fall pared steeper losses earlier in the day of as much as 14%. Nvidia has closed above the $4 trillion market cap benchmark since July 2025.

Chief Financial Officer Colette Kress will serve as interim CEO until a new leader is appointed, Nvidia said in a Thursday statement. Harvey C. Jones, former CEO of Synopsys, will become the first-ever chair of the board.

On Sunday, Huang admitted to having an affair. The CEO was romantically involved with Alice Huang, an executive at cloud company Megaspeed, according to The Financial Times, who U.S. intelligence officials believe is a spy for China.

Alice Huang is also tied to Nvidia's biggest competitor in China, Huawei, and may have owned stakes in Chinese businesses benefitting Huawei while simultaneously doing business with Nvidia, CNBC's Eamon Javers exclusively reported Thursday.

The New York Times on Wednesday reported that Huang had another extramarital relationship with Kimberly Powell, vice president of health care at Nvidia. Powell reports to Huang, according to the Times. Huang also had an "intimate relationship" with an unnamed second employee, Reuters reported Thursday.

Before Huang resigned, several Wall Street analysts told CNBC that this departure could lead the stock to fall 20%, given how critical he has been to Nvidia's success. Nvidia's stock is already down more than 20% over the last month.

Bernstein on Thursday slashed its price target on Nvidia's stock by half from $400 to $200.

Stacy Rasgon, a senior analyst at Bernstein, told CNBC's "Squawk Box Asia" on Thursday that Huang's alleged affair with a direct report means it is "much tougher for the board to leave him in position."

Huang was central to Nvidia's rise, having co-founded the company in 1993 and was pivotal in transforming Nvidia from a gaming hardware player to the company at the center of the artificial intelligence boom.

Rasgon said it is unclear who could be the "visionary" to lead Nvidia into the future as new CEO.

"Jensen is Nvidia, right? He is the visionary. The reason they're in the position that they're in today is because he made decisions 10 years ago," Rasgon said. "Who is the right person now to make the decisions for the next 10 years? I don't intuitively know who that person would be."

Earlier, U.S. lawmakers had called for Huang's resignation, adding further pressure to the CEO and the company.

Richard Reyle, chief investment officer at Questar Capital Partners, said Nvidia's lack of a succession plan makes the stock "uninvestable."

"Nvidia doesn't have that person in place, and so it's going to be either a choice of keeping Jensen and being faced with months and months of being hauled in front of probably congressional boards and things to explain themselves and all kinds of interrogations," Reyle told CNBC's "Access Middle East."

"Or they're going to have to move on from him, and that's just going to make the stock uninvestable. That doesn't make the AI trade uninvestable, but it certainly makes Nvidia uninvestable."

#2893. Minnesota Timberwolves: First Quarter Conferences

As a former teacher, conferences were always a bit of a mixed bag. On the hard side were the additional four hours of work tacked on top of a school day and the social anxiety of having the first in-depth conversations with parents, never fully knowing what kind of energy they would bring to the conversation. However, whether a student was struggling or thriving, the majority of the time, the conversations were very beneficial in getting everyone on the same page.

Each conversation really came down to the three main points:

The question of "Is there anything I can be doing as a teacher to better support them?"

In our season-long grading project, this is the first opportunity to track progress and get a look at how the categories, statistical ranges, and grade direction are shaping up. These stats reflect the first 11 games of the Minnesota Timberwolves' season, so the sample size is small, but large enough to begin to get a sense of the good, the bad, and to see if there are better ways to capture what each player is doing on the floor in their role.

1. These grades are roles-based, so the stats I'm looking at for each player are different and meant to highlight their team role.

2. Roles on the team can change as the roster and playing time changes—I will alter or add statistical categories throughout the season as needed. Especially in a preseason article, the expectation is that some of these initial stats will change as we get to know the team better.

3. The grade ranges and their correlation to stats are educated speculation based on previous career averages, team role, and minutes expectation. Like the categories themselves, if there is a range that is clearly missing the eye test, expect some tweaks as the season goes along.

First off, the fact that you are well on your way to a perfect attendance award is remarkable. While the season is long and nobody is going to count any chickens before they hatch, going 11/11 in games played deserves an immediate gold star. Well done!

You know what else deserves a gold star? The team offense. Despite losing the team's most important offensive cog, you've helped hold things together and guide the squad to the seventh-best offensive rating in the league, a number that, wherever it ends up after thirty-ish games, will probably be the floor of the season.

Now let's talk about rebounding. Honestly, it turns out this may just be a bad stat to track for you on this team. The team is rebounding fantastically to start the season as other players have stepped up. I mean, if you are an English major, who cares about your math scores, right? So maybe we need to focus on something else going forward…

Ah, yes. There it is. Four turnovers a game. 5.2 turnovers per 36 minutes, which would be the second-worst in the NBA for rotation players. That number is getting alarmingly close to being one that could soft-launch a bakery. Let's clean that up a bit and bring it closer to your career average of 3.2 going forward, okay?

*Sigh* There isn't much that can be said with just a four-game sample size of numbers, so I'll just use this space to opine for a moment.

This Wolves team is the most excited I've been about a group since the 23-24 squad had their meteoric rise and won over our hearts and minds with defensive grit and intensity. This season's squad is flashing that kind of defensive performance too… but only with an offensive ceiling to mirror it, assuming a healthy Anthony Edwards.

Teams that have both a top-five offense and defense often play a lot of basketball deep into the month of May and beyond. Get healthy soon, Ant!

Don't tell the other students, but you continue to be my favorite. Efficient shooting from deep and midrange, and being a key component of the number one defense in the league? Night in and night out, this team knows exactly what it is going to get out of you and, quite frankly, it's phenomenal.

Sometimes you have a conference that is short and sweet because nothing about the student's effort and production needs to be changed. Keep doing what you are doing, and we'll do everything we can to get you your first All-Star trip!

Welcome to Minnesota, Mr. Kuminga! Much like Jaden McDaniels, your work on the defensive end has been astounding. It is a wholly un-Wolves-like experience to go into matchups with the variety of power forwards there are in the league and feel as though you have a matchup for them. Raising your defensive EPM to an 86th percentile defender is JUST what the doctor ordered for this roster.

Here's the curious thing – you are shooting 40% from deep and yet have a near career low in true shooting percentage? Let me check the numbers here… ah okay. There it is. 52.5% from the free throw line. Have you been spending too much time with Mr. Gobert outside of class? As a career 70% shooter from the line, let's clean that up a bit and you'll see your grade in the "A" range in no time!

Let's talk about the good first – you are the anchor of the number one defense in the league through 11 games. For everything else that we are going to talk about, that's the most important thing, and it's the reason that you are on this team. You are one of the greatest defenders to ever play the game, and once again, anchoring a top defense in the league is truly a special career achievement.

It is really important that you understand how much we all appreciate your defense before anything else is said, okay?

But my God, has the goatee grown so thick that it is affecting your peripheral vision and ability to hit a layup? A non-rookie season career-low of 50% from the floor? Just 1.7 dunks per game despite having one of the best passing point guards in the league? This was supposed to be an apex offensive Gobert season!

I should note that your turnover numbers are at a non-rookie season career low… which is something… but that something may be YOUR INABILITY TO CATCH A PASS, INCREASING EVERYONE'S TURNOVERS AROUND YOU!! If the league tracked fumbled passes, you'd be having A WILT CHAMBERLAIN-ESQUE RUN!!!

Okay, I just had to get that out of my system. It had been brewing for a while, and conferences are a time for honest feedback, right? Plus, my therapist told me I should let it out so I'd stop mumbling "catch the damn ball, Rudy" in my sleep. Please re-read the first paragraph to know what is most important, and then let's just be better on the offensive end, okay?

An "A-" grade that mostly reflects the slow-cooking EPM metrics. If you were to keep consistently playing the kind of basketball you are, that EPM will be in the green in no time!

That points-per-shot-attempt number continues to be elite, and if this NBA season plays out similarly to previous ones, it would be a 95th percentile number, which is remarkable considering the increased offensive load and defensive focus. That PIE number (player impact estimate) would put you inside the top forty players in the league – quite the value for a player making $19 million this season.

Since the Anthony Edwards injury, no one has upped their usage more and with that, you've been the team's leading scorer over the past seven games, averaging 21.6 points per game. Turns out the "As You Wish" bench superstar is just a fantastic basketball player.

A B+ grade in your role to start the year because you are doing EXACTLY what is needed. Incremental steps of improvement to earn minutes, flashing an elite skill (offensive rebounding), and utilizing hustle to minimize mistakes and earn the coaching staff's trust.

Would I like to see a few more blocks per game? Sure, but it is clear that part of your game is being intentionally restrained to help you stay out of foul trouble as you keep getting your feet wet. Like a good stew, we're just going to keep letting you cook in a limited role that will pay dividends later in the season.

Before we begin, I need to ask you a question. Have you been turning in your "finishing at the rim" homework? Because the numbers here tell me that we're actually on pace to finish worse than last season's 52.9%, which was an area of improvement we had marked over the summer.

You're thriving within the concept of team defense (a huge jump from the preseason's -1.1 mark!), and you are getting to the rim and drawing fouls at more than twice the level of any time in your career. Talk about excelling in your role!

The only thing that is missing is a bit more of that deft touch around the basket to complement your elite downhill first step. Far be it from me to try to activate a healthy inter-team competition, but here's where the rest of the roster stands when finishing within 5ft of the basket:

Maybe everyone on the team should be spending more time with Trey Lyles. Speaking of…

I'll be honest with you, Mr. Lyles – when you transferred here, the only real sense of your skill set I had was a player who could space the floor with shooting and not much else. It is a testament to how wrong I am that the shooting really hasn't come around yet, and you are being a player who is consistently driving winning.

The defensive rating and defensive EPM (1.1) are really encouraging. In fact, that EPM number is fourth on the team, only behind Gobert, McDaniels, and Kuminga!

Know what else has been surprising? Your ability as a rebounder and, dare I even say it, a dunker?! Like most Timberwolves fans, these first 11 games have invoked a lot of "I was not familiar with your game" utterances. Start hitting those threes, and you could be hunting the ever-elusive A+ grade.

Let's talk about the scoring. That 25.1 points per thirty-six minutes is astounding. It would put you within the top twenty-five of the league on a list that includes mostly current NBA All-Stars. That you are doing it from more than just beyond the arc is the other really encouraging thing! This Wolves team needs a flamethrower scorer while Anthony Edwards is out, and you've stepped up to be just that.

I cannot explain the pace number being so low, but I am keeping it for now as a stand-in for the rest of your game. Despite the scoring inferno, you are the only rotation player to have a negative net rating (-10.1), a 15-point swing from the next closest rotation player. Because of this, your EPM numbers have dropped more than anyone else on the team, which means there is probably quite a bit lacking on the defensive end. An elite offensive game with a bad defensive game will land you comfortably in that "C" range.

I really don't want to jinx this, so I'm just going to whisper it between us, okay?

… Did you know that you are shooting 38% from deep this season on over two attempts per game? If this were to be maintained, when partnered with your defensive splash/hustle plays, it may turn out that there is a real and consistent role in the rotation for you. A player who has a limited role, steps into it, and goes beyond to earn more minutes? That is a role-based "A" if I've ever seen one!

A video of Jaylen Clark doing a Jaylen Clark thing is a perfect way to close out these first conferences because it encapsulates so much of what we've loved about this team so far. I'll be back in ten games with the finalized first-quarter report cards!

#2891. Jensen Huang's Nvidia exit casts shadow over Wall Street’s $500 billion AI financing bet

Leadership change at Nvidia has the potential to threaten financing of the artificial intelligence boom and test Jensen Huang's $500 billion vision to turn AI chips into tradable debt securities.

The Nvidia CEO's resignation was announced Thursday following revelations that he had two extramarital affairs — one with an employee and the other with a woman believed by U.S. intelligence to be a Chinese spy.

Shares in the tech giant — which has become central to the AI build-out in recent years — initially plunged more than 13% following Huang's resignation, before paring losses.

Nvidia has never identified a successor to Huang, who has led the chipmaker since its founding in 1993. Colette Kress, chief financial officer, has been named interim CEO.

Follow CNBC's live coverage of Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang's resignation

The company has been dubbed the "central bank" of the AI build-out, with Huang seen as the "hypeman" for the sector and its chips considered the industry's gold standard.

Huang's departure raises a central question: whether his successor can carry forward a financing strategy designed to transform GPUs from rapidly depreciating hardware into bankable infrastructure assets

The $4.2 trillion firm's industry dominance, with Huang front and center, allowed it to sign memorandums of understanding with some of Wall Street's biggest asset managers, intended to secure third-party capital for a landmark $500 billion AI financing drive.

The preliminary agreements, involving Apollo Global Management, BlackRock, Blackstone, Brookfield, Goldman Sachs, and KKR, sought to tap institutional investors and private capital to finance a range of computing infrastructure, including data centers and chip factories. The funding would involve specialized financing vehicles, with Nvidia's AI chips used as underlying collateral, in arrangements comparable to real estate or toll roads securitizations.

The financing arrangements are still preliminary and subject to final agreements.

BlackRock and Goldman Sachs declined to comment on today's resignation. The other firms weren't immediately available for comment.

The arrangements could now face heightened scrutiny in the aftermath of Huang's departure.

Sunaina Sinha Haldea, global head of private capital advisory at Raymond James, said a leadership change gives every firm involved a "natural moment to pause and look at the terms again."

"The best thing a new CEO can do is leave these partnerships alone in the first few months and focus on getting the final agreements signed," Haldea told CNBC via email. "Jensen brought these firms to the table with his vision, and any potential successor will keep them there by showing that the business behind that vision hasn't changed."

She added: "I don't think the capital walks away, because these lenders are underwriting Nvidia's chips and the customer contracts behind them, but they could want to be paid more for the uncertainty."

Charu Chanana, chief investment strategist at Saxo, said Huang's successor would need to reassure lenders that Nvidia's technology roadmap, customer relationships and strategic vision remain intact.

"I wouldn't expect Nvidia's financing partnerships to unravel simply because Huang leaves, but a leadership shake-up could make Wall Street more cautious, potentially slowing commitments or tightening financing terms," Chanana told CNBC via email.

"The bigger risk is a ripple effect across AI financing, particularly when rising debt, circular financing and uncertain investment returns were already under scrutiny," Chanana added. "This is not a financing crisis yet, but it could become a catalyst for a broader repricing of risk across the AI ecosystem."

Haldea added that she expects to see more discipline among investors in the aftermath of the scandal. "Lenders will ask harder questions about who is really carrying the risk in these structures, and that scrutiny will spread throughout the AI infrastructure deal market."

#2889. How Hockey Proves Class and Sportsmanship Still Have a Place in Professional Sports

It is the only major team sport that allows fighting. Hockey is played with helmets, sticks and blades. Players are checked, slashed, tripped, hooked and held. It is a game defined by blood on the ice, toothless competitors and violent collisions. And, oh yeah, it might just be the last bastion of class in professional sports.

In a sports world where Pat McAfee is swears, Joe Buck and Troy Aikman are openly at odds, and Jaylen Brown refuses to shake hands with longtime teammate Jayson Tatum; hockey is actually carrying the torch for dignity in athletics.

I watched the 2026 Hockey Hall of Fame Induction Ceremony this past week, which was simulcast on NHL Network and TSN. The inductees list included star after star after star.

Patrice Bergeron, a 19-season Boston Bruins center, multiple award winner and 2011 Stanley Cup champion. Cindy Curley, a women's hockey trailblazer who scored a record 23 points at the 1990 IIHF Women's World Championship. Carey Price, the winningest goaltender in the storied history of the Montreal Canadiens.

Pekka Rinne, a Nashville Predators icon and the winningest Finnish goaltender in NHL history. Keith Tkachuk, a tough yet skilled player who helped establish the power forward position. Brian Burke, the gruff, hard-edged executive and television commentator.

The ceremony offered a clear demonstration of why class remains alive and well in the National Hockey League. But it certainly isn't the only reason.

Let's start with the surface stuff. Many of today's sports television personalities dress like fans who just got escorted out of the arena. NHL play-by-play announcers and color analysts, however, take a different approach. These men and women are almost always look the part in sport coats, fine suits or dresses. High dollar shirts and ties.

Their attire is more suited for a fashion show than a faceoff.

In 2020, when the erudite Mike 'Doc' Emrick decided to retire as the lead play-by-play announcer for the NHL on NBC, he left a void in the hockey broadcast booth. Thankfully, that void has been filled by the likes of Kenny Albert, Sean McDonough, Eddie Olczyk, P.K. Subban, Kevin Weekes, Steve Levy and many others. Each bringing their own elegance and professionalism to the role.

Conversely, the NFL has gone in a different direction. Personalities like McAfee and ESPN's Jason Kelce know football. But their personalities and broadcasting styles are often loud, boisterous and animated. Moreover, sports media has become a place where excitement often surpasses experience.

Another example of what separates the NHL from other professional sports is its postseason. During the NHL playoffs, officials tend to call fewer penalties. Thus allowing these incredibly strong athletes to skate harder and play a more physical game.

What happens at the end of every playoff series? Two teams that just spent four to seven games beating the living heck out of each other meet at center ice and shake hands. That's right. The same hand that formed a fist and made a violent connection with an opponent's jaw is the one that reaches out to that opponent with grace and sportsmanship.

Perhaps tennis, where players meet at center court after a match and shake hands. But nothing compares to getting smacked in the face by a piece of hard rubber traveling at 100 mph, especially when the alternative is getting nicked in the knee by a wayward fluorescent green Dunlop.

There is a long-running belief in sports media that hockey players are among the most accommodating athletes when it comes to interviews and media availability. We see plenty of clips of coaches and athletes in other sports ducking reporters or being downright rude to them. In turn, NHL players tend to be available and accommodating.

They seem to have retained the seemingly lost qualities of politeness and cooperation.

The 2026 Hockey Hall of Fame Induction Ceremony showcased both sides of hockey. You had players like Bergeron and Price, soft-spoken beacons of class who thanked their teammates. Shunned the spotlight and essentially gave everyone else credit for their brilliant careers.

Then you had Burke, who entered in the Builder category. The Minnesota native's speech was more like a Don Rickles roast. At one point, he ran through the numerous teams, organizations and television networks he had worked for and joked, "A lot of thanks for people who fired me. I don't know why I'm doing it."

Each of the Hall of Fame inductees devoted more time to thanking others than congratulating themselves. Isn't that refreshing in today's world of look-at-me clicks, likes and follows?

The NHL has always set the pace when it comes to identifiable players. A lunch-pail attitude and a basic gentility that somehow coexists with bone-crushing hits and fisticuffs. Moreover, the sports world needs these dignified degenerates now more than ever.

These aren't NBA players who embrace load management. Or even baseball pitchers who consider 75 pitches or five innings a complete game. Hockey players don't break a nail and miss six weeks. They break a bone and return by the middle of the second period.

Perhaps no one exemplifies the class-and-crash dichotomy of the NHL more than 2026 Hall of Fame inductee Tkachuk. In addition, he ranks 36th all-time in goals with 536 and 41st in penalty minutes with 2,219. During his induction speech, he summed up the singular beauty of the NHL with a simple statement: "Our culture is the best in sports."

Barrett Media produces daily content on the music, news, and sports media industries. Sign up for our newsletters to stay updated and get the latest information right in your inbox.

The post How Hockey Proves Class and Sportsmanship Still Have a Place in Professional Sports appeared first on Barrett Media.

#2888. United Airlines won’t let AI agents book your flight. Expert says you shouldn't let a bot do that for you, anyway

United Airlines said Wednesday it would be restricting artificial intelligence bots from its digital platforms, warning that allowing third-party AI assistants to access account credentials could expose sensitive personal information, lead to unintended purchases or other unauthorized account activity. The move is similar to Amazon's recent blocking of Meta's Muse agent from making purchases on its site.

Still, AI agents can complete a wide range of tasks on your behalf. Instead of just asking Muse what the weather will be tomorrow, you can tell it to suggest an outfit based on tomorrow's weather forecast and book you a rideshare if it's going to rain, for example. Or, rather than just creating an itinerary for your next vacation, you can connect a payment option and have your Dot — OpenAI's agent offering — book flights, hotels and excursions for you.

You can even have an AI agent buy and sell stocks through investing platforms like Robinhood.

"You can have agents do interesting things for you, like categorize your spending, or flag unusual charges, or model what-if scenarios," he says. "Those are good things, but bad things would be moving money, making trades, filing forms. All those things should stay manual at this point."

AI-enthusiast traders may need some convincing, though.

Here's what you should consider before outsourcing your whole to-do list to an AI agent.

Though AI agents' capabilities are improving, they still operate in a fairly literal sense, which can create problems, Ghose says. If you tell your agent to book you the cheapest flight, for example, it may book you a flight at 3 a.m. or one with three layovers, unless you give the agent specific guardrails.

"The bot only knows your context," Ghose says. "It cannot infer, as we humans are able to do, because of instinct and culture and education."

Further, bots can still get it wrong even with a seemingly clear and direct command. They "treat all data as being equal," Ghose adds. So if it finds information on the web that is either inaccurate or subjective, it could give you misleading guidance or fail to meet your standards for the given task.

For example, if you tell your agent to book you a reservation at a good barbecue restaurant, it may get you a table at a place that claims to be "the best barbecue in the world" on its website. But the restaurant may be in an inconvenient location or have poor reviews elsewhere on the web. Your command may need to be more specific, like asking your agent to check reviews on Google Reviews or Yelp.

Depending on what task you've assigned the bot, the outcome could be rather innocuous. But when individuals consider letting bots make purchases for them, make changes to their investment portfolios or and the like, the result could be more consequential.

"In the end, you want to ask yourself: Does the human need to be in the loop?" Ghose says.

If you're booking a vacation, for example, it's reasonable to ask an AI agent to help make a decision on your destination based on the weather, your budget and other factors. But actually booking the flights and hotels "should still come back to me," Ghose says. He wants to maintain final say over major financial decisions.

Doing so may even save you money. In Booking Holdings' third-quarter earnings report, the Connecticut-based travel technology company reported 20% higher spending among customers who used AI assistants versus those who didn't to book accommodations on Agoda, one of its travel platforms. It's unclear whether those customers were led to pricier options or did, in fact, seek more expensive amenities. The company doesn't cite a single factor for the finding.

AI agents are also making their way into the healthcare sector, which can pose risks for users too quick to rely on the agents' advice, Ghose says. Letting an AI agent handle prescription refill requests, for example, could be convenient, but AI managing prescriptions more broadly may act on "incomplete records and won't be able to recognize a dangerous interaction or a worsening condition before the action has already taken effect," he says. Generally, a clinician will have to sign off on any new prescriptions, but federal agencies are backing programs that would allow AI agents to assist in diagnostics and prescription management for patients.

For some tasks like investing decisions, you need to have a certain level of expertise to clearly dictate your strategy and goals. And even if you give yourself final say on trades, instead of delegating to the bot, it's essential that you understand the risk involved in moves your AI agent proposes. While a large language model can help you research investments and gather information around an asset, humans are still better at putting that into the context of their individual financial strategy and goals, AI experts say.

Agents can "make your trading experience easier, there is no doubt about it," Shridhar Rathore, an experienced investor who uses agentic trading tools, recently told CNBC Make It. "But [letting agents] make investment decisions, that's where people can lose money if they are not careful...they have to make sure that for the execution, the human is in the loop."

"You want to be really specific and say, 'I am crafting a trading strategy, and these are the kind of things I'm trying to optimize for,'" like whether you're thinking about short or long-term strategy, Ghose says. "So that as [the agent] ingests information, it can treat it with the right context."

#2887. Junk bonds are 'flashing yellow.' Watch these warning signs

Cracks are forming in the junk bond market as investors demand higher payouts for owning the market's riskiest debt. It isn't time to ditch high-yield bonds, but investors should pay attention to the warning signs.

High-yield bonds now yield 8.1%, up from 7.22% a month ago. The increase reflects a jump in yields across the curve as investors bake in more inflation from high energy prices and other pressures, including concern about the deficit — hitting nearly $2 trillion in the fiscal year that ended Sept. 30.

The high-yield market is also showing stress on the credit side with spreads recently widening to levels not seen since April, according to the Federal Reserve Bank of St. Louis. Credit spreads are the difference in yield between the bonds and Treasurys of similar maturities. Wider spreads mean investors are demanding higher yields for holding corporate debt, viewing it as riskier.

Spreads are at 315 basis points in the overall high-yield market, higher than a year ago but still below levels in March when they reached 346 bps. One basis point equals one one-hundredth of a percent, or 0.01%.

The high-yield market consists of bonds rated BB+ by S&P and Fitch and Ba1 and under by Moody's. The lowest-rated cohort, CCC and below, has seen the most movement with spreads climbing dramatically over the past year to roughly 1,250 bps.

Right now, the high-yield market is "flashing yellow" but is "far from red," said Michael Arone, chief investment strategist at State Street Investment Management.

It makes sense that investors are demanding more compensation for taking on additional credit risk as borrowing costs rise, he said.

Yields are elevated across the board with the 10-year Treasury reaching its highest level since 2002 earlier in the week.

"The bigger question is whether this is simply a repricing of interest rate risk, or the beginning of a more fundamental reassessment of credit quality," Arone said.

He's in a wait-and-see mode since earnings are still growing, interest-coverage ratios remain good, and while default rates have ticked up some, he believes it is not concerning.

That said, the starting point in spreads is likely weighing on investors' psyche, since they are still low by historical standards.

"There's a small margin of error here, which I also think raises the anxiety level," Arone explained. "The compensation that investors are receiving for taking on this credit risk isn't overwhelming relative to history, and therefore subtle changes in credit spreads can be concerning."

While there may be concerns about part of the lower-rated market, the overall high-yield market is in good fundamental shape.

In fact, credit quality is at a record high, with BB bonds making up over 60% of the market compared with 38% prior to the global financial crisis, said Kelley Gerrity, a fixed income strategist at Morgan Stanley Investment Management.

"We've had higher-quality companies coming in, and with higher rates now, you also have more discipline from companies that are more indebted ... just because of the higher cost of capital, so that actually is creating a bit of a healthier picture as well," she said.

The lowest tier of high yield has always been more speculative, since the bonds have a higher default risk. Therefore, it's no surprise that they are the ones seeing the most widening in spreads, said Collin Martin, head of fixed income research and strategy at the Schwab Center for Financial Research.

The overall move in high-yield spreads have been "orderly," he said. "Are there cracks forming? I think the cracks are logical cracks with the lowest-rated bonds and it's too early to say that it's spreading to the broad credit market."

In addition, fluctuations within the CCC-rated and lower spreads have been idiosyncratic, Gerrity added. Morgan Stanley recently broke down the spreads in the lowest cohort into two buckets: performing assets and non-performing assets, which are defined as spreads above 1,000 bps.

The non-performing spread to worst is 2,818 bps, while the performing bucket — the biggest piece of the CCC market — is 461 bps, she said.

"It is isolated," she said. "I'm not necessarily sure that that will go away anytime soon, but it doesn't tell the story of overall concern and flashing caution for credit markets right now."

For now, investors should remain selective within higher yield, Gerrity advised.

"We're looking for the best relative value opportunities, and we think that exists within that single-B cohort, and so we're leaning into that," she said.

Investors should be concerned if there is a steep widening in the broader high-yield market.

But there's scant evidence of stress in the BB cohort. Spreads in that group are at 194 bps, up from 179 bps a year ago — although the move hasn't been in a straight line.

"We're going to be focusing our attention on what are considered some stronger businesses," Martin said. "If we start to see the markets demand higher spreads there also, that's what we'll be looking at to see if risks are really rising."

It's also important to understand that the Federal Reserve is raising rates in a strong economic environment, said R.J. Gallo, chief investment officer of global fixed income at Federated Hermes. The central bank is concerned about an inflation problem amid high fossil fuel prices due to the Iran war, while growth has been stronger than expected, he noted.

"With growth being good as the reason that the Fed is hiking, then you wouldn't expect high yield to blow out because growth means revenues stay up, means cash flow and profitability stays up," he explained.

High yield becomes a disaster when the economy is heading into a sharp economic downturn, Gallo said.

"That's when spreads really widen out," he said. "But a recession is not the odds-on bet. Now, talk to me in six months if the Fed keeps hiking over and over, if oil prices stay high for longer, well, then maybe we will start to wonder."

#2886. Steph Curry’s defense of Ayesha exposes the ugly double standards of the sports manosphere

If Steph Curry is leading with his full government name, know that he means business. In a 90-second video released on Wednesday, from the Instagram account of "Wardell Curry", he put the internet trolls who have been attacking his wife for years on notice. "Usually the noise doesn't bother me, doesn't bother Ayesha," the four-time NBA champion and Paris Olympic knockout artist said. "But when you're trying to step on the accomplishments of my wife, and feel the need to have to jump in and give an opinion on something that you really don't know about, [it] kind of speaks to the bigger conversation on how you view women in this world and what they're allowed and what they're not allowed to do."

Posterity will remember this as the NBA's Oscar slap moment, but without the unnecessary violence. A bizarre turn in the parasocial compact when the restless fanboys pressured a superstar into publicly defending his wife. On this occasion, Curry had to defend his wife for the heinous crime of being the leading lady in a Christmas romcom: a Hallmark knockoff called Second Chance Holiday in which Ayesha Curry plays a dating expert with bad holiday luck and shares a kiss with a man who is not Wardell Stephen Curry II – her husband of 15 years and father to their four children. That apparently was a bridge too far for some of Curry's former NBA peers.

Related: Yaxel Lendeborg is the NBA's most entertaining rookie – and he's not holding back

Sports gossips have been making a game of picking apart Steph and Ayesha's relationship since he shot to stardom at Davidson College. They seem to forget that while Steph was stealing hearts as the NCAA Tournament's baby-faced bracket buster, Ayesha was carving out a solid career in Los Angeles as a working actor, with guest spots on Disney's Hannah Montana and on CBS's Gary Unmarried opposite comedian Jay Mohr – who is now the other half of LA Lakers president Jeanie Buss, incidentally.

Steph Curry defends his wife, Ayesha, against criticism of her on-screen kiss in a video posted to Instagram on Wednesday.

The critics blanched when Ayesha joined the 2010s culinary craze with a blog and a YouTube channel, in which she shared dangerous, subversive recipes such as "Stephen's five-ingredient game-day pasta". When the hustle mushroomed into a full-blown lifestyle brand – with signature kitchenware and cookbooks, a Food Network show and a landmark CoverGirl deal – the gossips filleted Ayesha for daring to upstage her more famous husband, the real Chef Curry.

No matter what Ayesha did, it seemed, there was no winning over sports fans steeped in the manosphere's retrograde views of women. They didn't approve of her sharing bikini photos online or her body language in snaps with Drake or her candor about the struggle to hold on to her sense of identity and autonomy after marriage and motherhood. The point was driven home yet again when ex-NFL players Emmanuel Acho and LeSean McCoy, perhaps misunderstanding the concept of acting, debated the Curry kiss on their macho podcast – with Acho suggesting that Ayesha, through her words and actions, keeps telegraphing to the world that "she's not that into Steph Curry".

Intimacy work has become a wedge issue in Hollywood, with a number of prominent actors saying they will no longer participate in kissing or sex scenes – forcing some directors to get creative. But the Curry speculators aren't arguing about the mechanics of making movies – a point Steph himself hastened to rebut in his video statement. They say this stuff as if they know the Currys. They don't. They say this stuff as if they're also still with their teen sweethearts. They're not – and Acho especially is one to talk. They say this stuff as if audiences might also interpret Ayesha's on-screen kiss as an act of adultery. They won't. They'll just let the 78 minutes wash over them as they fold laundry or scroll through their phones.

No matter what Ayesha did, it seemed, there was no winning over fans steeped in the manosphere's views of women

These manosphere brains insist on typecasting Ayesha while giving other prominent athlete spouses plenty of runway. The recent marriage of Coco Jones, the Grammy-winning singer and star of the Fresh Prince update Bel-Air, and Donovan Mitchell, engine of the Cleveland Cavaliers, is treated as an equal pairing. The same goes for Major League ace Paul Skenes and his gymnast-influencer girlfriend, Livvy Dunne. When Travis Kelce started dating Taylor Swift, the talk was all about how he, the Kansas City Chiefs and the NFL stood to benefit. And when Kim K and her sisters take up with high-profile athletes, the manosphere brains roll their eyes, chalk it up to the game and blame any poor performances that might result on the Kardashian curse.

But when it comes to Ayesha, there is no room for self-reflection, growth or individuality. She is, and will always be, an NBA wife in the eyes of many. Her tiresome critics would much rather see her on the sideline managing the kids than see Steph gathering them up to bask in the afterglow of his biggest moments. Weirdly, the tradcasting doesn't stop with Ayesha. Steph Curry's mother has been dealing with the same gender policing since she divorced his father, a former NBA player, in 2021 and remarried last month. That all this outrage is building at a time when gender roles in sports have become a culture-war battleground shouldn't be lost on anyone.

Ultimately, though, the relentless snooping into the Curry marriage reveals a deeper insecurity about Steph. Here's a God-fearing, wholesome family man who speaks up for female empowerment and walks the talk when he's not burying clutch threes – and he's still getting the job done for the Warriors at age 38.

Curry has spent his career making doubters rue the day they said he was too small for pro ball, and that same bracing self-belief has helped him thrive away from the court as a venture capitalist, Hollywood producer and Bay Area philanthropist. Through 18 NBA seasons, he has remained free of scandal, extramarital or otherwise – no mean feat for an NBA superstar, much less a Silicon Valley giant. And that just makes the sports and celebrity hounds who are only following along for the potential of a Will Smith – or Tiger Woods – like train wreck push harder in their relentless pursuit of dirt.

But for dedicated knuckle-draggers like Arenas and his cohort, who still don't see an alpha male when they behold Curry, it isn't just that he could be with pretty much anyone else, given his wealth and fame. It's that he chooses Ayesha and that there still isn't a rift between them to be found, even after all these years of trying.

The armchair analysis of the Currys' marriage isn't likely to stop anytime soon. But it's a stargazing exercise in the end, with vast distances between the picture in view and the elusive reality. The constant churn of speculation only serves to show the willful ignorance at play, particularly when it comes to the difference between an on-camera kiss – again, Ayesha was acting – and an act of infidelity. With his forceful social media defense, Steph didn't just offer a reminder of what true manhood looks like. He delivered a message that should sound awfully familiar to Ayesha's haters – and give them pause: Keep my wife's name out your mouth.
