#2884. Jensen Huang's exit from Nvidia raises critical questions about the future of AI

Michael Dell described Jensen Huang as a "visionary of the AI age." Deutsche Telekom CEO Tim Höttges said a photo with the Nvidia CEO produced more media coverage for his company than its iPhone launch. During the keynote of his annual Dreamforce conference, Salesforce's Marc Benioff said, "Jensen, we're all following you, bro," as the two paced through the audience.

Follow CNBC's live coverage of Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang's resignation

His fame extended way beyond tech. In May, Huang joined President Donald Trump on Air Force One for a trip to China to meet with President Xi Jinping. Three months later, Huang gathered six of the biggest names at the Nasdaq to announce on CNBC a proposed $500 billion artificial intelligence buildout plan, cementing his central role at the intersection of Wall Street and Silicon Valley.

As Nvidia became the first company to rip past a $5 trillion market cap, bending the laws of physics with its advanced graphics processing units and pushing the AI boom to seemingly endless bounds, the now 63-year-old Huang, clad in a black leather jacket, traversed the globe as the face of the brand powering the next industrial revolution.

Over the past week it all came crashing down. Following two reported extramarital affairs — one with an alleged Chinese spy and another with an employee — Huang is leaving the company he co-founded 33 years ago in crisis, and thrusting the AI market into chaos. The board is now left to pick a successor for a company that's been run by a singular figure with no second-in-command. Chief Financial Officer Colette Kress has been named interim CEO. Harvey Jones has been appointed chairman.

Nvidia has not responded to multiple requests for comment.

"How companies handle a crisis determines their future," former Cisco CEO John Chambers, now a startup investor, told CNBC's "Fast Money" on Wednesday. "How they handle this will determine if they're a great company and a great leadership team beyond one person."

It had been a tumultuous stretch for Nvidia, even before the Huang saga led the board to launch an investigation of the CEO's behavior and the potential national security implications. The stock is on pace for its sixth straight weekly drop, which would be the longest losing streak since 2012. That includes a 9% slump last week after the midterm elections resulted in sweeping Democratic victories in both chambers of Congress, and the passage of dozens of ballot measures that could slow the buildout of new data centers.

Nvidia is scheduled to report quarterly results on Tuesday, and expectations remain high, with analysts projecting 92% year-over-year revenue growth to $109.2 billion, according to LSEG. That's up 18-fold from the same quarter in 2022, just before OpenAI launched ChatGPT and kicked off the generative AI era.

It's a historic level of growth for any business, and particularly notable for a company that spent most of its first few decades as a niche technology provider for video games.

Huang founded Nvidia to make chips for computer graphics, both for video games and rendering. In the early 2000s, he and other executives saw an opportunity to put GPUs to work for computationally intense projects. That technology eventually turned Nvidia into the most important player in AI and a key American asset for national defense.

In the process, Huang became the ultimate decider, choosing how, when and where GPUs get allocated. The biggest buyers were the hyperscalers — Amazon, Google, Meta and Microsoft — which needed the chips to build their own AI services and make them available to cloud customers. OpenAI, meanwhile, was snapping up GPUs for its powerful models and consumer apps.

To expand his customer base, Huang designated compute capacity to a number of so-called neoclouds like CoreWeave and Nebius, putting equity into the companies and helping them build big businesses around renting out GPUs.

Oracle gobbled up billions of dollars worth of GPUs, going heavily into debt in the process. Elon Musk's xAI, now part of SpaceX, did the same to build its Grok model, before eventually starting to rent out excess capacity to Anthropic and Google.

Huang wanted the world standardizing on Nvidia. Last year he demanded that Oracle buy additional Nvidia networking hardware if it wanted allocation to its state-of-the-art Grace Blackwell chips, said two people familiar with the deal who asked not to be named because the negotiations were private.

In August, Nvidia said it will provide up to $105 billion in financing for an OpenAI data center in Ohio that could be the largest of its kind in the world.

"He created the product that started the industry," said Chris Miglino, CEO of neocloud Axe Compute. "Without his leadership we may be many years behind where we are today with AI chip technology, which is the driving force behind the neocloud market."

When it comes to investing cash in fledgling startups, Huang is known to host entrepreneurs at Nvidia's Silicon Valley headquarters. While there could be 30 Nvidia employees in the room for the pitch meeting, only Huang asks questions, according to people with knowledge of the matter, who requested anonymity because they're not authorized to speak publicly about it.

One source told CNBC that key aspects of project finance are so reliant on Huang's sign-off that, when he travels, those involved in the deal are forced to wait for his plane to land before learning if they can go forward or if terms have to be renegotiated.

Huang's ambitions extended well beyond the U.S. He wanted to be a force in China, which became a difficult market for Nvidia during the Biden administration and was virtually off-limits after Trump implemented strict export controls in his second term. Huang's aggressive pursuit of China may have been his undoing.

The woman, with whom the Nvidia CEO had a romantic affair, was identified by U.S. intelligence as having been recruited by China's Ministry of State Security to gather information on Huang, according to people briefed on the intelligence cited by The New York Times. She has business ties to Megaspeed International, a Singapore-based cloud computing firm and an Nvidia customer that's facing scrutiny over suspicions that it facilitated billions of dollars of restricted Nvidia chips to China.

U.S. lawmakers are up in arms, with many demanding investigations as well as Huang's resignation. Senate Banking Committee member Kevin Cramer, R-N.D., vocalized his concerns on Wednesday.

"What bothers me at the end of it is what we don't know is what she knows about him that can be utilized," Cramer told CNBC, right before Huang resigned and referring to the affair. "This is a guy that's right now so vulnerable to extortion, blackmail. I just don't think, as a national security matter, we can run the risk."

But industry experts are quick to emphasize how important Huang was to the health and growth of the broad AI ecosystem. He had the leading names on Wall Street on speed dial as he tried to turn GPUs into what he called an "investable asset" with an expanded roster of financial backers, and he had personal relationships with all the key figures in AI like OpenAI's Sam Altman and SoftBank's Masayoshi Son.

On the deal-making side, Huang's top lieutenants were Kress and Vishal Bhagwati, head of corporate development, sources said, though they add that all the big decisions were made by the CEO. And when seeking a financial backstop, the person behind Huang was Nico Caprez, the CEO's son-in-law and the company's vice president of global AI infrastructure growth, three people said.

Executives at numerous Nvidia customers, partners and portfolio companies told CNBC that Huang frequently emailed or called CEOs to offer his help in challenging times.

Tomás Puig, founder and CEO of enterprise AI startup Alembic, said that following the collapse of Silicon Valley Bank in 2023, his company was "down to weeks of cash and Nvidia was our biggest customer."

"I emailed Jensen, and they prepaid two years of invoices so we could keep the lights on and people kept their jobs," Puig told CNBC.

That approach persisted, as recently as this week. On Monday, a day after publicly apologizing for being unfaithful to his wife, Huang emailed the CEO of AI infrastructure startup Fireworks AI, according to a person with knowledge of the matter. Fireworks, which Nvidia first backed in 2024, was dealing with a series of high-profile cyberattacks.

Huang, whose name was suddenly front-page news for all the wrong reasons, was checking in, the person said, just to see if he could help.

— CNBC's Lora Kolodny, Jordan Novet and Seema Mody contributed to this report.

#2883. Why is Zac Taylor answering for Duke Tobin's Bengals mistake? | Opinion

Duke Tobin apparently knew exactly how he wanted the Bengals' failed trade explained.

The Bengals' personnel chief sent his head coach before reporters Wednesday to convey his apology for the aborted acquisition of Philadelphia Eagles pass rusher Arnold Ebiketie. Taylor said Tobin had given him explicit instructions about how to present what happened.

Jason Williams: Cincinnati Bengals big week turns bad at NFL trade deadline

Then, Taylor offered a revealing introduction: "I'm not comfortable doing it, but I'm going to do what he asked."

Consider that arrangement. The personnel department makes a mistake. Its leader determines how the mistake should be portrayed. The coach delivers the explanation, acknowledges his discomfort and faces the questions.

Tobin was not made available to reporters. Apparently, taking responsibility can be delegated by Bengals management.

The Bengals reportedly agreed to send a sixth-round draft pick to Philadelphia for Ebiketie. But acquiring his contract also would have canceled the projected third-round compensatory pick associated with Trey Hendrickson's departure in free agency.

Over the Cap analyst Nick Korte publicly flagged that consequence. Shortly afterward, the Bengals backed out. NFL Network reported that the compensatory-pick issue caused the reversal.

Backing out was the right decision. Understanding the full price before reaching an agreement would have been better.

Taylor conveyed that the transaction proved more costly than initially anticipated and that the problem was on Cincinnati's end. That leaves an obvious question: How did the personnel operation get that far without accounting for a third-round pick?

Tobin should answer it himself. We have been here before with his absence.

After the November 2025 trade deadline, Enquirer columnist Jason Williams reported that the Bengals had not made Tobin available to explain their inactivity or answer for the roster's problems. Taylor was left to handle the questions while the executive responsible for assembling the team remained silent.

This week brought a particularly awkward variation: Taylor was expected to explain a front-office failure according to instructions from the front office.

Coaches answer publicly for play calls, clock management, preparation and losses. Players stand at their lockers and explain missed assignments. The personnel chief should be able to explain a transaction his department failed to complete.

Korte, meanwhile, deserves acknowledgment.

In 2024, he questioned Cincinnati's compensatory-pick award following Jessie Bates III's departure. The NFL subsequently acknowledged its own calculation error and upgraded a sixth-round selection to the third round. That was a league mistake, not another failed Bengals trade. But Korte's analysis again proved valuable to Cincinnati.

The Bengals have not confirmed whether Korte's post alerted them to the problem, though it has been reported that it did. If so, a public thank-you would be appropriate.

More Dennis Doyle: Reds need to pay Sal Stewart before his price goes up | Opinion

Fans deserve more than an apology delivered through the coach. They deserve to know what went wrong and what will change.

He should have stepped forward to explain how it happened. That's what good organizations do.

Dennis Doyle lives in Anderson Township and is a member of the Enquirer Board of Contributors.

#2882. In a policy switch, the Fed says its October 2027 meeting will not see a vote on interest rates

The Federal Reserve won't vote on a potential interest-rate change at its October 2027 meeting, a departure that is part of a broader initiative from Chairman Kevin Warsh aimed at reducing the central bank's market footprint.

According to an announcement Thursday, the rate-setting Federal Open Market Committee will set aside its Oct. 26-27 session for "the discussion of broad monetary policy strategy issues."

Warsh announced the move at his news conference after the FOMC met in October, though he did not specify which meeting would be affected.

In 2027, there will be seven voting meetings, with the October session set up as extra time for policy deliberation without a formal vote or post-meeting statement. The new calendar shows that the final meeting of the year will now take place in November. It had previously been set for December.

"Now, what's the reasoning behind this change? To me, it's quite straightforward," the chairman said in explaining his rationale. "Getting policy right involves assessing near-term developments at timely, regular, and information-rich intervals."

"But it's equally important we reserve time and space, as an intellectually diverse group, for strategic deliberations—for thinking anew about big questions, looking out over longer time horizons, focusing, as I say, on the left side of the decimal point," he said.

Warsh has been critical of the Fed's prior communications strategy, saying policymakers spent too much time providing guidance on policy moves that handcuffed them when it came time to make decisions.

Under typical practice, the Fed meets eight times a year, following with a vote on interest rates and then a news conference from the chair.

Since Warsh took over, the post-meeting statement has been shortened dramatically. Moreover, the press question-and-answer sessions have been cut to around half an hour from their customary 45 minutes or so.

Warsh said in October that the meeting schedule is another step in his plan to streamline communication. He indicated last month that the number of nonvoting meetings would increase to two in 2028.

"I believe the setting of the meeting schedule and agenda is more than some administrative detail," he said. "It's an institutional choice, affecting the nature and the quality of our deliberations and, ultimately, the wisdom of our decisions. It's yet another change to get the conduct of monetary policy on the right track."

The FOMC next meets Dec. 8-9. Its first meeting in 2027 will happen Jan. 26-27.

Correction: The Fed's October 2027 meeting will not see a vote on interest rates. The headline in an earlier version misstated the year.

#2881. What changed? Rams make stunning Puka Nacua reversal just one day after McVay’s assurances

On Wednesday, Los Angeles Rams head coach Sean McVay insisted the team had no plans to place star wide receiver Puka Nacua on injured reserve, citing what he called "roster semantics."

By Thursday afternoon, those semantics apparently went out the window.

The Rams announced they were placing Nacua on IR after all, guaranteeing their leading receiver will miss at least four games as he recovers from knee and toe injuries suffered during Sunday's 20-13 victory over the Washington Commanders.

"Puka [Nacua] with his knee/toe. We're not going to put him on IR because of some semantics with the roster and things like that, but he still is going to be out for an extended period of time," McVay said Wednesday. "It wasn't like any sort of new information."

Apparently, the roster math changed overnight.

Los Angeles Rams wide receiver Puka Nacua (12) warms up before a game against the Arizona Cardinals at SoFi Stadium in Inglewood, Calif., Sunday, Oct. 18, 2026. IMAGN IMAGES via Reuters Connect

Los Angeles Rams wide receiver Puka Nacua reacts after winning an NFL football game against the Washington Commanders in Landover, Md., Sunday, Nov. 8, 2026. AP Photo/Stephanie Scarbrough

Nacua will now be sidelined through the Rams' Dec. 13 showdown with the San Francisco 49ers, making their Dec. 20 matchup against the Dallas Cowboys his earliest possible return.

To fill the vacancy, Los Angeles promoted wide receiver Brennan Presley to the active roster and signed Tyler Scott to the practice squad.

But the decision carries consequences beyond losing Stafford's favorite target for the next month.

Under NFL rules, teams are limited to eight injured reserve return designations during the regular season. The Rams have already used three and are expected to need four more for Davante Adams, Kobie Turner, Terrance Ferguson and Nacua.

That would leave just one available designation for the remainder of the season.

Justin Dedich, Dean Connors and Jordan Whittington are also on injured reserve, meaning the Rams could eventually face some uncomfortable decisions about who gets another opportunity to play.

They could also preserve that final designation as insurance against another injury.

For a team already battered by injuries and desperately trying to keep its Super Bowl aspirations alive, every roster spot has now become precious.

#2880. The Lakers’ belief in Sandro Mamukelashvili is paying off

This offseason, the Los Angeles Lakers invested a sizable amount in Sandro Mamukelashvili by inking him to a four-year, $52 million deal. Through three weeks, that's been a remarkable move as Mamukelashvili's absolutely thriving with the Lakers and providing critical, versatile minutes off the bench.

The Georgian big man is averaging 10.8 points, 4.5 rebounds and 1.6 assists per game while shooting 41.2% from deep and 57.7% on 2-pointers. Los Angeles entrusts him with varying roles, whether it's spacing the floor, operating as a playmaking hub, driving closeouts or toggling between power forward and small-ball center. It's evident the organization, whether it be players or coaches, have the utmost confidence in him.

Now, the goal is to make sure Mamukelashvili himself embraces that autonomy and is unburdened by any fear of mistakes whenever he's on the floor.

"I want him to understand how much his teammates, his coaches, myself, we believe in him. The focus on the next play, I think, is big for him," head coach JJ Redick told reporters following Wednesday's win, when Mamukelashvili recorded 23 points, three rebounds, two assists and one steal. "Being able to move on after a missed shot, a defensive mistake, whatever it may be, just move on to the next play and focus on that. But I think the big thing for him is understanding that he has a lot of freedom."

Mamukelashvili said he feels the unbridled support rippling from coaches and teammates and is optimistic he'll soon echo that credence in him. But he admitted achieving it is easier said than done.

"I hope I'll start believing in myself as much as they do," he told reporters Wednesday.

Mamukelashvili's finest two games of the season may have come Wednesday in Milwaukee and Monday against the Atlanta Hawks. During those two outings, he's scored a season-high 23 points each time and averaged 5.5 rebounds, 2.5 assists and 1.0 steals. As he continues to grow more comfortable in his new environment, performances similar to those could become the norm — much to the Lakers' benefit.

"He's just getting more confident as he plays more. He's an extremely, extremely talented offensive player," Walker Kessler said Wednesday. "We all believe in every shot he shoots and every play he makes. … We believe in his offensive game and he's getting more confident and he's really, really helping us."

#2879. Donald Trump Jr. exits advisory role at Kalshi prediction market

Prediction market platform Kalshi ended its advisory contract with Donald Trump Jr. in response to his venture capital firm, 1789 Capital, also holding a stake in rival platform Polymarket, according to Kalshi's spokesperson.

Conversations on Trump Jr.'s exit began in August after 1789 Capital announced a major stake in Polymarket, the spokesperson said. It was a mutual arrangement, with both Kalshi and Trump Jr. agreeing it was a conflict of interest to keep the president's son as an advisor while serving as a top investor for Polymarket, the spokesperson added.

Trump Jr.'s departure comes after Democrats secured a majority across the House and the Senate, with the party expected to crack down on the prediction market industry. While Trump Jr. has exited Kalshi, he still serves as an advisor to Polymarket. His ties to the industry could make prediction markets an easy target for lawmakers to go after, Joseph Konizeski, a former trial attorney in the CFTC, previously told CNBC.

In early October, Kalshi CEO Tarek Mansour said on MS NOW that Trump Jr.'s connection to the company creates an "optics issue," but added that it has never relied on that relationship to gain favor with the government.

Kalshi first named Donald Trump Jr. a strategic advisor for the company in January 2025.

Disclosure: CNBC and Kalshi have a commercial relationship that includes customer acquisition and a minority investment.

#2878. Why is Zac Taylor answering for Duke Tobin's Bengals mistake? | Opinion

Duke Tobin apparently knew exactly how he wanted the Bengals' failed trade explained.

The Bengals' personnel chief sent his head coach before reporters Wednesday to convey his apology for the aborted acquisition of Philadelphia Eagles pass rusher Arnold Ebiketie. Taylor said Tobin had given him explicit instructions about how to present what happened.

Jason Williams: Cincinnati Bengals big week turns bad at NFL trade deadline

Then, Taylor offered a revealing introduction: "I'm not comfortable doing it, but I'm going to do what he asked."

Consider that arrangement. The personnel department makes a mistake. Its leader determines how the mistake should be portrayed. The coach delivers the explanation, acknowledges his discomfort and faces the questions.

Tobin was not made available to reporters. Apparently, taking responsibility can be delegated by Bengals management.

The Bengals reportedly agreed to send a sixth-round draft pick to Philadelphia for Ebiketie. But acquiring his contract also would have canceled the projected third-round compensatory pick associated with Trey Hendrickson's departure in free agency.

Over the Cap analyst Nick Korte publicly flagged that consequence. Shortly afterward, the Bengals backed out. NFL Network reported that the compensatory-pick issue caused the reversal.

Backing out was the right decision. Understanding the full price before reaching an agreement would have been better.

Taylor conveyed that the transaction proved more costly than initially anticipated and that the problem was on Cincinnati's end. That leaves an obvious question: How did the personnel operation get that far without accounting for a third-round pick?

Tobin should answer it himself. We have been here before with his absence.

After the November 2025 trade deadline, Enquirer columnist Jason Williams reported that the Bengals had not made Tobin available to explain their inactivity or answer for the roster's problems. Taylor was left to handle the questions while the executive responsible for assembling the team remained silent.

This week brought a particularly awkward variation: Taylor was expected to explain a front-office failure according to instructions from the front office.

Coaches answer publicly for play calls, clock management, preparation and losses. Players stand at their lockers and explain missed assignments. The personnel chief should be able to explain a transaction his department failed to complete.

Korte, meanwhile, deserves acknowledgment.

In 2024, he questioned Cincinnati's compensatory-pick award following Jessie Bates III's departure. The NFL subsequently acknowledged its own calculation error and upgraded a sixth-round selection to the third round. That was a league mistake, not another failed Bengals trade. But Korte's analysis again proved valuable to Cincinnati.

The Bengals have not confirmed whether Korte's post alerted them to the problem, though it has been reported that it did. If so, a public thank-you would be appropriate.

More Dennis Doyle: Reds need to pay Sal Stewart before his price goes up | Opinion

Fans deserve more than an apology delivered through the coach. They deserve to know what went wrong and what will change.

He should have stepped forward to explain how it happened. That's what good organizations do.

Dennis Doyle lives in Anderson Township and is a member of the Enquirer Board of Contributors.

This article originally appeared on Cincinnati Enquirer: Why is Zac Taylor answering for Duke Tobin's Bengals mistake? | Opinion

#2877. Inside India newsletter: Trump's poll setback fuels hope for U.S.-India trade deal by year end

Hello, this is Priyanka Salve, writing to you from India.

Welcome to the latest edition of "Inside India" — your one-stop destination for stories and developments from the world's fastest-growing large economy.

After months of being weighed down by high energy prices, trade uncertainty and foreign investor exodus, India has had plenty to celebrate this Diwali. Foreign investors returned to its market in November and, now, it seems the long-awaited U.S.-India trade deal could be around the corner.

Experts say that the two sides are closer to signing on the dotted line following the U.S. midterm election results than they were before.

Any thoughts on today's newsletter? Share them with the team.

The U.S.-India trade deal, which has been riddled with uncertainty since February, is likely to get a final push to the finish line, thanks to U.S. President Donald Trump's electoral setback in the midterm polls.

Experts told CNBC that Trump could lean toward closing some of the pending trade deals to promote them as policy achievements to the American public, and the pact with New Delhi is a "prime candidate."

They added that Trump and Indian Prime Minister Modi are likely to sign the trade pact when they meet in December for the G20 leaders meeting, as negotiations have largely concluded.

The U.S.-India trade talks "are far along and there seems to be a gentlemen's agreement between Trump and Modi" that the deal should be concluded when the two meet at Mar-a-Lago in December, Mark Linscott, former assistant U.S. trade representative and senior advisor at the U.S.-India Strategic Partnership Forum, told CNBC.

Trump has used tariffs as a pressure tactic to clinch trade deals with several countries, even as that approach has drawn the ire of his political opponents who have flagged that export levies have increased costs for American households. Cost of living was the top concern for American voters in the midterms.

"The election was a verdict on the cost of living, and tariffs are now a liability the President cannot defend on the numbers," Ronak D. Desai, visiting fellow at Hoover Institution of Stanford University, told CNBC.

"Congress will not hand him an affordability win, but a trade deal is one he can announce on his own," Desai said, adding that an announcement of a deal with India is "more likely than it was before the election."

India's commerce and external affairs ministries and the U.S. Trade Representative's Office did not respond to CNBC's requests for comment.

When asked about how U.S. midterm election results will reshape the ties with New Delhi, a spokesperson for India's Ministry of External Affairs on Tuesday said that the relationship between the two countries "enjoys bipartisan support in the United States."

Trump insists Americans do not share the burden of his tariffs, but according to estimates from Tax Foundation, a nonprofit, the duties raised the average U.S. household's tax burden by $820 in 2026 and $1,000 in 2025.

As a result, the Democrats, within days of taking control of both the houses of Congress are preparing to challenge Trump's signature tariff policy. A trade deal at this time could be seen as a validation of Trump's tariffs-led approach to business.

"The president may lean more into getting deals done – seeing them as policy achievements that he can promote to the American public," Atman Trivedi, partner at Washington-based DGA Albright Stonebridge Group, told CNBC. He added that the Trump administration would like to get a deal done with India by the G20 leaders' summit in December.

While American voters are more affected by import duties on Canadian goods, experts said that a trade deal with India could blunt criticism about tariffs as an effective bargaining chip.

In February, Trump announced a deal with India, claiming that New Delhi had agreed to "Buy American" at a much higher level after the U.S. agreed to slash tariffs to 18%. Trump had earlier imposed a 25% tariff on imports from India, along with an additional 25% duty on goods from New Delhi for buying Russian oil.

But that deal has since been awaiting final approval, especially after the Supreme Court struck down Trump tariffs, giving India space to demand preferential tariffs that give it an edge over competitors.

While American and Indian officials at the start of October said that the negotiations had reached an impasse, Trump on Oct. 20 said that the two sides were "pretty close" to a final deal. As per Indian media reports, the country's Commerce Minister Piyush Goyal confirmed that discussions on the trade deal had largely "concluded."

"We have been hearing for weeks that a deal is close to be settled," Aryan D'Rozario, associate fellow and chair on India and Emerging Asia Economics at the Center for Strategic and International Studies, told CNBC, adding that Modi's visit to the G20 summit in December "will provide the perfect stage to formalize an agreement."

India as an AI hedge? The country's stocks could see robust inflows as markets globally tankThe Indian stock market is emerging as an attractive hedge as investors seek refuge from a brutal sell-off in the artificial intelligence trade, making it a strong contender for global capital. If global investors "neutralize" their underweight positions on India, it could lead to foreign inflows of $115 billion, as per JPMorgan.

India billionaire Adani's firm buys Dutch firm Evos to gain foothold in EuropeAdani Ports and Special Economic Zone, India's largest port operator, will acquire Netherlands-headquartered Evos from Igneo Infrastructure Partners in an all-cash deal at an enterprise value of 2.56 billion euros.

Nov. 12: India CPI inflation for October.

Nov.15: India unemployment rate for October.

#2876. Top Wall Street analysts prefer these 3 dividend stocks for stable income

Uncertainty in the Middle East, elevated Treasury yields and fears about an AI bubble continue to keep global markets on edge. Given this volatile backdrop, investors seeking stable income can consider enhancing their portfolios by adding dividend-paying stocks.

However, the vast universe of dividend stocks can make selection challenging. Tracking the ratings of top Wall Street analysts can help shortlist attractive dividend stocks, as their recommendations are backed by in-depth research.

Here are three dividend-paying stocks that are highlighted by Wall Street's top pros, as tracked by TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Chord Energy is an independent exploration and production company with long-lived assets primarily in the Williston Basin. With a quarterly base dividend of $1.30 per share, CHRD offers a dividend yield of 3.67%.

In a research note on third-quarter expectations, RBC Capital analyst Scott Hanold reiterated a buy rating on CHRD stock with a price target of $175. "We model production near the top of guidance and continue to see strong performance from new wells and base production," said Hanold.

Specifically, the five-star analyst expects Q3 2026 production at 283 thousand barrels of oil equivalent per day, or Mboe/d, in line with the Street's consensus and near the high end of the 278 to 284 Mboe/d guidance. Hanold said his Q3 2026 earnings per share estimate increased to $5.29 due to lower non-cash depreciation, depletion and amortization.

Meanwhile, cash flow per share estimate moved lower to $13.59 to reflect Hanold's update based on the current backdrop and commodity mark-to-market. He expects Chord Energy to report capital spending of $375 million for Q3 2026, slightly above the Street's estimate of $369 million.

Overall, Hanold remains bullish on CHRD, given its best-in-class balance sheet. He expects a free cash flow payout of 80% in the third quarter, with the possibility of an upside. Hanold expects investors to focus on management commentary on 2027 capital plans, Williston M&A opportunities, and long-term performance of 4-mile wells.

Hanold ranks No. 310 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 63% of the time, delivering an average return of 14.9%. See Chord Energy KPIs on TipRanks.

Williams, an energy infrastructure company that delivers natural gas, is the next dividend pick. The company recently completed the $5.5 billion acquisition of Momentum Midstream, which will expand its natural gas infrastructure in the Haynesville to meet growing Gulf Coast LNG, power and industrial demand. At a quarterly dividend of $0.5250 per share, Williams offers a yield of 2.89%.

In her latest research note, RBC Capital analyst Elvira Scotto reiterated a buy rating on Williams stock with a price target of $87. Ahead of Q3 2026 earnings, the analyst said that she expects WMB to announce strong quarterly performance.

Scotto raised her estimates to reflect insights from the quarterly catch-up call, the completion of the Momentum Midstream acquisition, and mark-to-market quarter-to-date commodity prices. She expects WMB to report Q3 2026 adjusted earnings before interest, taxes, depreciation and amortization of $2,036 million, which is modestly above the Street's consensus estimate.

Furthermore, Scotto expects Williams' Transmission, Power & Gulf segment to increase quarter-over-quarter, driven by the startup of the Socrates power project and favorable natural gas liquids, or NGL, margins.

Scotto noted that WMB stock has underperformed due to backlash related to data centers and investor anticipation of another Power Innovation project. She expects the company to announce at least one more project before the end of this year, potentially with a new customer and a contract duration of 15 to 20 years, which would be a positive catalyst for the stock.

Scotto ranks No. 175 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. Her ratings have been profitable 67% of the time, delivering an average return of 16.2%. See Williams Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

EOG Resources, a crude oil and natural gas exploration and production company, will hold its third-quarter earnings call on Nov. 6. At a quarterly dividend of $1.02 per share, EOG offers a dividend yield of 2.75%.

Heading into Q3 earnings, Jefferies analyst Lloyd Byrne reiterated a buy rating on EOG Resources stock and increased his price target to $185 from $175. The analyst expects the company's Q3 cash flow per share to exceed the Street's consensus by 13%, driven by better-than-expected production.

Byrne expects crude oil production, excluding the UAE, to reach 551 Mboe/d, which is at the high end of guidance (546-551 Mboe/d) and above the Street's projection of 548 Mboe/d, driven by continued growth in the Utica.

Furthermore, Byrne expects cash operating expenses to come in at $10.27 per barrel of oil equivalent, below the midpoint of guidance of $10.05 to $11.35, helped by lower lease operating expenses. Overall, the analyst expects EOG to deliver free cash flow of $2.65 billion, supporting another quarter of buybacks near the minimum 70% shareholder return threshold and implying about $1.5 billion in share repurchases.

"As a first indication of the '27 plan, we expect EOG will message another year of mid-single-digit (~3.5%) growth on a flat capital budget (~$6.55bn), which is above the Street modeling 1% oil growth at $6.6bn," said Byrne.

Byrne ranks No. 303 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 58% of the time, delivering an average return of 18.5%. See EOG Resources Statistics on TipRanks.

#2872. Cramer says Jensen Huang betrayed Nvidia shareholders. How to pick up the pieces

CNBC's Jim Cramer criticized former Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang on Thursday for putting the chipmaker's future at risk.

"Did he betray you, the shareholders?" Cramer asked. "Yes. End of story."

Huang resigned Thursday morning following reports of an affair with an Nvidia executive who reported to him. Hours later, Reuters reported that he had an "intimate relationship" with a second employee. These revelations came days after Huang admitted to an affair with an executive at a Singapore-based cloud computing company whom U.S. intelligence agencies suspect of spying for China. Longtime CFO Colette Kress was named interim CEO of Nvidia. Cramer said Huang's departure was necessary to protect Nvidia from further fallout.

Shares ended Thursday down more than 4%, but at its lows of the morning, the stock was off over 14%. The decline in Thursday's session brings Nvidia's losses this year to 12%.

While questions remain about what comes next for the AI chipmaker, Cramer argued that Nvidia's future extends beyond the executive who helped build it into one of the world's most valuable companies. "There won't be a better Nvidia, just a different one [without Huang]," Cramer said, envisioning a company that is better organized and more disciplined about its investments.

Under Huang, Nvidia built an extensive equity investment portfolio that included a $5 billion investment in Intel in 2025 and a $2 billion investment in AI cloud provider CoreWeave earlier this year. The investments, and many others, were part of Huang's strategy to strengthen Nvidia's position across the AI industry.

During the CNBC Investing Club's Morning Meeting livestream Thursday, Cramer recalled urging Kress to reduce the company's stakes in Intel and CoreWeave, only to be told that Huang preferred to maintain them. Cramer suggested that new leadership could take a more disciplined approach to capital allocation, potentially cashing in on some of those investments and returning the proceeds to shareholders through even bigger stock buybacks.

Cramer expressed confidence in Kress, praising her deep understanding of Nvidia's operations and financials. He argued that the longtime finance chief is well positioned to provide stability as the company navigates the investigations surrounding Huang, which stretch from the boardroom to Washington, D.C.

Nvidia didn't respond to CNBC's request for comment on Cramer's remarks. Heller, the crisis PR firm Huang reportedly hired as early as last year, did not respond to CNBC's request for comment.

Going forward, Nvidia's underlying business remains strong, Cramer said, pointing to its substantial cash reserves and expectations for a robust earnings report Tuesday evening. But he cautioned that even strong quarterly results might not be enough to reassure investors during this time of lingering uncertainty.

Investigations into Huang's conduct and possible violations of U.S. chip export restrictions could result in sanctions that hurt Nvidia's international business. Meanwhile, competitors, including Advanced Micro Devices, are making progress with their own AI chips, potentially threatening Nvidia's market dominance. AMD shares surged almost 6% on Thursday, extending their winning streak to five straight sessions.

Those risks have prompted Cramer to take a more cautious approach to Nvidia stock, particularly after the substantial gains shares have delivered over the years for Cramer's Charitable Trust, the portfolio of 32 stocks managed by the Club. On Tuesday, as Huang's future at Nvidia looked more and more in doubt, the Club downgraded Nvidia to a hold-equivalent rating.

"Don't ever turn a successful investment into an unsuccessful trade," he said, urging longtime shareholders sitting on big gains to consider taking some profits. "You don't want to give up all of your gains if the stock falls further. It fell further today after many of the analysts jumped ship and downgraded the stock from buy to hold. There could be more ahead."

At the same time, Cramer cautioned against giving up on Nvidia altogether, pointing to the company's extensive engineering expertise and multiyear product roadmap as reasons to believe it can continue competing at the forefront of AI without Huang.

The "Mad Money" host also drew parallels to Apple's leadership transition from Steve Jobs to Tim Cook, suggesting that Nvidia could benefit from a similarly capable successor who brings a different management style without sacrificing the company's technological edge.

#2869. Panthers’ Jaelan Phillips gets concerning injury downgrade before Saints game

Panthers' Jaelan Phillips gets concerning injury downgrade before Saints game appeared first on ClutchPoints. Add ClutchPoints as a Preferred Source by clicking here.

The Carolina Panthers' defensive blueprint for their Week 11 matchup against the New Orleans Saints on Sunday could require adjustments following the latest update on outside linebacker Jaelan Phillips. After participating fully in Wednesday's practice, the pass rusher was downgraded to limited on Thursday's injury report because of his lingering hamstring issue.

Phillips' reduced workload comes after he missed three consecutive games with the injury. He initially suffered the problem in Week 4 versus the Detroit Lions before aggravating it during his first game back against the Philadelphia Eagles following the bye week. Carolina has since taken a cautious approach with the defensive playmaker.

His absence has affected the Panthers' ability to generate pressure. Phillips recorded 2.5 sacks and six quarterback hits across his first five appearances. However, Carolina managed just one sack and four quarterback hits throughout his three-game absence. Patrick Jones II accounted for the team's only sack against the Denver Broncos last week.

Cornerback Will Lee III (hamstring), who missed last week's game, returned to full participation, as did starting cornerback Mike Jackson (groin).

The Panthers will be hoping Phillips can return soon, given the difference his pass-rushing production could make to their defense.

(Photo by Mitchell Leff/Getty Images) | Getty Images

They don't really shoot (or make) threes, partly a result of swapping Paul George for a more downhill player in Brown, and they don't generate extra opportunities by crashing the offensive glass. They're also surprisingly low in assists, especially considering the playmaking gifts of James and, to a lesser extent, Embiid, owed no doubt to some issues with spacing, a coach not exactly known for offensive ingenuity, and a lineup that's still largely unaccustomed to playing together.

It also doesn't help that Embiid is….well, if not cooked, then certainly in the process of being marinated. His recent injuries seem to have sapped him of his mobility and, while his skill is beyond question, you don't really ever want to see a 7-footer hitting just 37% of his shots from the field (nor a ghastly 12.1% from three), albeit in a small sample size.

They're also a bit thin aside from having a few useful perimeter players off the bench; their only actual backup centre is Adem Bona, who is shooting a whole percentage point better than Embiid from the field. Needless to say, they could use a boost on the interior. It's great news for the Raptors, who are a bit lacking in interior size themselves, and who aren't an especially good defensive rebounding team (23rd in the league in defensive rebound rate).

The Sixers have been a pretty solid defensive team though, with the seventh-best defensive rating in the league, and, while Embiid may not be able to switch onto guards anymore, he's still a smart defender who can be an obstacle in the paint. His declining lateral quickness is less of a disadvantage of a team like Toronto that doesn't space the floor exceptionally well.

These Sixers are very different from last year's team and this will be the first time they face off against the Bron-and-Brown version of Philly. That being said, the two teams split the season series last year, 2-2, with both squads winning once at home and once on the road.

LeBron and Embiid vs various ligaments might be the true matchup of the game. Both players are listed as questionable on the NBA's official injury report, as is Adem Bona. If neither of them play, Toronto won't exactly be facing a murderer's row of big men on Thursday night, with the Sixers almost certainly having to call players up from the G League or play some very funky small-ball lineups to get by. Maybe we'll get 30 minutes of Tacko Fall. Who knows!

Besides that, the Raptors will likely need RJ Barrett to outduel Brown, and Immanuel Quickley, who struggled to score against Boston, to keep pace with Maxey. It's not quite the marquee matchup fans hoped for after both teams' major offseason moves, but it could be a fun one nonetheless.

#2865. Top Wall Street analysts are bullish on the growth potential of these 3 stocks

Fears around the artificial intelligence trade and higher pressure on oil prices are weighing on global markets, but a few stocks offer investors solid growth prospects even in choppy times.

Investors with a long-term horizon can look beyond the ongoing volatility and track the recommendations of top Wall Street analysts to pick attractive stocks with strong long-term growth potential.

Here are three stocks favored by some of Wall Street's top pros, according to TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Health insurer UnitedHealth delivered better-than-expected results for the third quarter of 2026. The company also raised its full-year earnings outlook, reflecting continued progress on its turnaround plan.

Following the Q3 print, Morgan Stanley analyst Erin Wright reiterated a buy rating on UNH stock with a price target of $529. "UNH's beat & raise reinforces turnaround momentum, with improving MA [Medicare Advantage]/Optum Health supporting a higher '26 EPS baseline & upside to '27 consensus," said Wright.

The five-star analyst added that better-than-expected performance in the UnitedHealthcare and Optum Health businesses supported another upgrade to the company's 2026 adjusted earnings per share outlook to at least $20.50. Wright noted that UNH raised the earnings guidance despite incremental investments planned for the fourth quarter of 2026.

Furthermore, Wright highlighted management's view that the consensus 2027 EPS of $22.63 was too conservative. Reaching even the low end of the company's 13% to 16% long-term EPS growth target would imply an EPS of $23.17.

Wright raised her 2026 EPS estimate to $20.65 from $19.82 and 2027 EPS to $23.24 from $22.54, reflecting the latest upside and an improving 2026 medical loss ratio to 87.4% from 88.1%. Overall, she believes that UNH's operational performance continues to improve, increasing her confidence in its UHC strategy and Optum Health turnaround execution.

Wright ranks No. 264 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. Her ratings have been successful 64% of the time, delivering an average return of 15%. See UnitedHealth Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

Social media giant Meta Platforms has been making headlines since the launch of its Muse AI agent, which is seeing solid traction. Investors look forward to updates on the company's AI strategy when it announces its third-quarter results on Oct. 21.

Heading into Q3 earnings, Guggenheim analyst Michael Morris maintained a buy rating on Meta stock and increased his price target to $850 from $700, reflecting higher estimates for 2027. He expects Muse usage momentum and engagement levels to shape investor sentiment into the Q3 earnings call.

Specifically, Morris highlighted that, according to Apptopia, Muse reached 1.3 million daily active users in five weeks versus 300,000 in the first week. Given that Muse's current adoption represents less than 0.05% of Meta's 3.6 billion daily active user base, the analyst expects management to prioritize boosting usage over near-term monetization.

"Over time, we believe Muse monetizes primarily through businesses paying to reach consumers through their agents, consistent with the economics of Meta's core advertising business, rather than consumer wallets," said the five-star analyst. He added that the checkout integrations with Shopify and PayPal reflect this framework.

While there are concerns that a rival could overtake Muse or the recently launched Muse Wonder and push Meta into a prolonged cycle of overinvestment, Morris remains bullish due to the company's strong record in scaling WhatsApp and competing strongly with TikTok via Reels as well as free distribution to an extensive user base.

Morris ranks No. 629 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 57% of the time, delivering an average return of 13.2%. See Meta Platforms KPIs on TipRanks.

Next is semiconductor company Marvell Technology. Earlier this month, Marvell held its Investor Day, where it raised its total addressable market estimate to $400 billion by 2030, reflecting solid AI-led demand tailwinds.

After hosting a non-deal roadshow with Marvell's senior management following the Investor Day, Stifel analyst Tore Svanberg reiterated a buy rating on the stock with a price target of $370.

The five-star analyst said that a major focus of the roadshow was the XPU and XPU Attach mix, as Marvell's Custom business expands toward its FY31 revenue target of $30 billion. In this regard, management reiterated that it expects a balanced mix of XPU and XPU Attach revenue in FY31, though it noted that either segment could grow rapidly enough to shift the mix toward 60/40 or 40/60.

While XPU drove most of the nearly $1.5 billion Custom revenue in FY26, Svanberg expects Marvell's XPU Attach business to grow substantially through the end of the decade, leading to a more balanced mix in this business. At the Investor Day event, the company raised its FY29 Custom target from more than $10 billion to over $12 billion, mainly driven by strength in the XPU Attach business.

Svanberg said that another key topic in the roadshow was whether Marvell can secure enough capacity to meet its raised targets. Management said that it met with suppliers ahead of Investor Day to share its five-year goals and received commitments to support these targets. The analyst believes that Marvell's supply chain commitments give it a competitive edge over rivals as customers seek reliable partners for large-scale infrastructure buildouts.

Svanberg ranks No. 91 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 58% of the time, delivering an average return of 31.8%. See Marvell Technology Statistics on TipRanks.

#2864. Steph Curry to miss at least three NBA Cup games with abdominal tear

It's still unclear how long that Steph Curry's oblique abdominal tear will keep him off the court, though it will be two weeks at a minimum. But however long it is, the absence of their star will likely prevent the Warriors from competing for commissioner Adam Silver's most treasured piece of basketball hardware: The Emirates NBA Cup.

I mention the sponsor out of the deep and abiding respect we hold for all of the NBA sponsors, and particularly Commissioner Silver's good friends in the United Arab Emirates. The Cup has taken the basketball world by storm since it began in 2023, delighting fans and gamblers alike with its focus on point differential, and reminding a nation to check the color settings on their televisions thanks to the garish special courts.

Our personal favorite is the Los Angeles Lakers "Forum Blue and Urine" design, which showcases Luka Doncic and Austin Reaves on a court that makes it look like the Crypto.com Arena itself is severely dehydrated.

The Warriors are 1-0 in Cup play, but Curry's injury means he'll miss Friday's showdown with the San Antonio Spurs, who lost to the New York Knicks in last year's Cup final, the most significant Finals loss to the Knicks they'd suffer all season. Yes, Charles Bassey is prepared to go toe-to-toe with Victor Wembanyama, having proved his mettle against Marcus Smart, but it may be tough for the rest of the team to match the firepower of the Spurs and Harrison "The Black Falcon" Barnes.

It's hard to imagine Curry will be back to face the Sacramento Kings or the Portland Trail Blazers in two Cup games bracketing Thanksgiving, when families will gather together and express their gratitude for the mid-season tournament. It's not impossible that the Warriors could be 3-1 after that, but 2-2 is a more likely record, which wouldn't get the Dubs the wild-card spot. Even if they did get in, Curry would only be three weeks past his injury for the knockout rounds Dec. 4 and 5, and the Cup Final, held in glamorous Indianapolis, Indiana, is just one month away.

This is the gamble the Warriors made when they assembled a veteran roster this year in hopes they could make a serious Cup run. Sure, they could try to trade Kristaps Porzingis for immediate help, but are teams really going to help a possible Cup opponent this close to the knockout round? Yaxel Lendeborg has been a revelation as a rookie, but he's never had to prove himself under the Emirates NBA Cup spotlight.

The Knicks became the first team to win the double last season, taking home the NBA Cup and the Larry O'Brien trophy as NBA champions. The Warriors had a chance to be the first NBA team to win the "treble" — that is, Summer League, Emirates NBA Cup, and NBA titles. (As much as Adam Silver would like there to be, there's still no Champions League for the NBA)

Sure, Curry has five months to get ready for the regular NBA playoffs, with Jimmy Butler and Moses Moody also set to return. But for the hardcore Emirates-heads out there, it's looking like another lost NBA Cup season for the Warriors.

#2862. Without Jensen Huang, Nvidia's organizational structure isn't 'built to last,' management expert says

Jensen Huang resigned Thursday as CEO of Nvidia, the chipmaking company he co-founded in 1993, following public revelations of his extramarital affairs. Colette Kress, Nvidia's chief financial officer, will serve as the company's interim CEO.

Huang's visionary leadership style and his central role in driving the company's strategy, combined with the company's unusually flat management structure, will make him especially difficult to replace, experts say.

Huang notably had more direct reports than the typical CEO, with a "direct staff" of approximately 60 employees, he said in a March 2026 interview with podcaster Lex Fridman.

"The more direct reports a CEO has, the less layers are in the company. It allows us to keep information fluid," Huang told CNBC's Andrew Ross Sorkin in 2023.

Having minimal hierarchy has worked for Nvidia so far "because [Huang] has been a strong leader with a strong vision," says Jo-Ellen Pozner, associate professor of management at Santa Clara University's Leavey School of Business.

But that structure could make the company's leadership shake-up even more difficult to navigate, she says.

At companies with flat management structures, the CEO often has even more influence than they would in a traditional hierarchy, according to Joseph Fuller, professor of management practice at Harvard Business School.

Nvidia's structure was "designed to adapt well in a world of change, either caused by us or affecting us," Huang said in a March 2025 conversation with Arthur Mensch, cofounder and CEO of Mistral.

Since he directly managed the company's top executives, Huang held "a tremendous amount of power" at Nvidia, Fuller says. "He's the orchestrator," he continues. "He's the coordinator. He's resolving the disputes. He's allocating the resources."

Now, the company has lost its top decision-maker, Fuller says.

Without Huang, "there is likely nobody who is able to coordinate among those various people with their different expertise and domains," Pozner says, which could lead to a slowdown in technical work.

There are some advantages to Nvidia's flat structure, says Denise Rousseau, professor of organizational behavior and public policy at Carnegie Mellon University. Having fewer management layers can strengthen "local leaders" in each area of the company, she says, which can help stabilize Nvidia through its leadership transition.

But "nobody else has the big picture view" that Huang did, Pozner says, which makes finding a permanent successor within the company especially difficult. Moreover, with dozens of direct reports, it's unlikely that Huang had the time to groom one of his subordinates to take over one day, she says.

Pozner predicts that Nvidia's next leader will steer the company toward a more traditional management structure. "I understand that a lot of people, especially in Silicon Valley, are averse to rigid hierarchies and the kind of structure that we expect from a legacy organization," she says. "But it's functional for a reason."

Ultimately, Nvidia's current system is "not an organizational structure that's built to last," Pozner says.

#2860. Why Nvidia stock drop isn't a big deal for many ETF, mutual fund investors

Nvidia stock plunged in early trading on Thursday after Jensen Huang stepped down as CEO. Huang co-founded the chipmaker 33 years ago and built it into a behemoth at the center of the artificial intelligence boom.

Since Nvidia is among the largest publicly traded companies by market capitalization, its stock is, by default, a substantial holding in several popular mutual funds and exchange-traded funds.

Many investors therefore hold the chipmaker's stock in their portfolios — and may worry about their exposure.

But mutual fund and ETF investors' exposure to Nvidia stock is likely relatively low, data shows — and any big swings in its stock price are likely to have a fairly muted impact on investor portfolios, financial advisors said.

"This is just a very contemporary, sensational case of risk," said Philip Chao, a certified financial planner and chief investment officer at Experiential Wealth, based in Cabin John, Maryland. "But that is part and parcel to stock investing."

Read coverage of CNBC's 2026 Financial Advisor 100 list, including stories featuring ranked firms and their advisors:

Nvidia's stock fell more than 10% early Thursday on news of Huang's resignation.

Huang stepped down following his admission to having an affair with Alice Huang, a woman who U.S. intelligence officials believe is a spy for China, and after reports that he had a separate extramarital relationship with a direct report, Kimberly Powell, vice president of healthcare at Nvidia. Reuters also reported Thursday that Huang had an "intimate relationship" with a second Nvidia employee, citing two people familiar with the matter.

The stock has since pared some of those losses, and was down more than 5% as of roughly 2:50 p.m. EST.

However, investors "shouldn't worry" about their exposure, Chao said.

For example, if Nvidia were 5% of an overall fund's holdings and the stock dropped 10%, the fund's value would decline 0.5%, assuming all else was equal, Chao said.

"It's not a big deal," he said. "Most people are just reacting to the sensational aspect of this [news]."

A flash poll of members of CNBC's Financial Advisor Council and Financial Advisor 100 on Thursday showed 18 respondents are planning to hold their Nvidia allocations steady in client portfolios. Three are increasing their stake, and six do not own the chipmaker. None of the respondents are decreasing their ownership. The online poll, conducted from 11 a.m. to 2:30 p.m. ET on Thursday, was sent to 122 firms and 27 responded.

Let's look at some of the most popular stock ETFs for some specific examples of why investors likely have limited exposure.

Nvidia stock is the largest holding in the Vanguard S&P 500 ETF (VOO).

The fund, as the name suggests, tracks the performance of the S&P 500 U.S. stock index and had a market capitalization of $1.7 trillion as of Sept. 30, according to the most recent Vanguard data.

On that date, Nvidia stock accounted for about 8.4% of the entire portfolio.

Assuming that weighting carried forward to today, a 5% decline in Nvidia's stock would only cause the fund to lose about 0.42% of its value, all else equal, according to an analysis by Experiential Wealth for CNBC.

A 10% decline would lead the fund to drop by 0.84%, according to the analysis.

The Invesco QQQ Trust (QQQ) is an ETF that tracks the performance of the tech-heavy Nasdaq-100 index.

The fund held about $473.8 billion as of Nov. 10, according to Invesco data.

Nvidia was its second-largest holding, at 6.8% of the overall portfolio. Apple was No. 1, at about 8.4%.

Based on that weighting, the fund would lose just 0.34% of its value if Nvidia's stock lost 5%, all else equal, according to the Experiential Wealth analysis. A 10% Nvidia loss would equate to a 0.68% loss for the overall fund.

Nvidia stock was the largest holding in the Vanguard Morningstar Total Stock Market ETF (VTI).

The fund had $1.8 trillion as of Oct. 31, according to Vanguard.

Nvidia stock accounted for about 7.13% of the fund, as of Sept. 30.

According to that weighting, the overall fund would lose roughly 0.36% of its value if Nvidia's stock lost 5%, all else equal, according to Experiential Wealth's analysis. A 10% Nvidia loss would equate to a roughly 0.71% loss for the overall fund.

"There's a difference between [stock] volatility and substantial losses you cannot recover," Chao said.

The Nvidia stock moves fall in the former camp, not the latter, he said.

Across all mutual funds and ETFs, "large growth" funds have the largest average weighting to Nvidia stock, according to Morningstar data. These are funds that tend to hold shares in fast-growing U.S. stocks with large market capitalizations.

Large growth funds had an average 10.34% weighting to Nvidia stock as of Wednesday, according to Morningstar. Funds that focus on the technology sector had a 7.31% average weighting to the stock, the second-largest exposure, it found.

Of course, Nvidia stock doesn't exist in a vacuum, financial experts said.

"A 10% Nvidia drop would usually pull down other semiconductor and AI-linked names, such as AMD ... and Micron," Chao said.

And that means mutual fund and ETF losses could be larger than Nvidia holdings might otherwise suggest, he said.

"This event could easily add further pressure to already stretched AI-related stock valuations," Renée Friedman, global head of research at EXANTE, a brokerage firm, wrote in an e-mail.

However, that's not a foregone conclusion, experts said.

The S&P 500 index and the Nasdaq Composite, for example, were down slightly for the day as of about 2:50 p.m. EST.

— CNBC's Sharon Epperson contributed reporting.

#2858. The Three Point Stance: Purdue (3-6) vs. #16 Iowa (7-2)

Purdue will travel to take on the 16th ranked Iowa Hawkeyes in an effort to salvage a season that at one point seemed very promising at 3-3 following victories against Illinois and Minnesota. Head Coach Barry Odom will take on defensive play calling duties following a Sunday afternoon announcement that he had fired defensive coordinator Kevin Kane following a season of continued errors and fundamental mistakes. To get a victory would mean a seismic shift from what Purdue has been over the last several weeks but not out of the realm of possibility from what they have shown at times this season.

Iowa enters this game as a team on the fringe of consideration of a College Football Playoff invitation. Sitting at 7-2 with remaining games against Purdue, Illinois, and Nebraska, Iowa's destiny is out in front of them to make their first appearance in the playoff under head coach Kirk Ferentz in his 28th season. The offense isn't anything to call home about but the defense continues to be one of the nation's best units under the widely considered best defensive coordinator in the country in Phil Parker.

When Ryan Browne has been given time to throw the ball this season, he has done a pretty good job of finding his playmakers at receiver. The improvement of those receivers from last year has allowed Browne to take a big leap in his development but that quickly goes away when he isn't given a clean pocket to work with. That starts up front with the offensive line that features some very promising but young players like Rico Schreiber, Brock Brownfield, Nuku Mafi, and Marques Easley.

Iowa's defensive front relies heavily on pressure from their front four led by players like Joseph Anderson (4 sacks), Bryce Hawthorne (3.5 sacks/8TFL's), Drew Campbell (2.5 sacks/4TFL's), and several other players. DC Phill Parker's main priority is to ensure explosive plays are limited and force offenses into long, sustained drives that end in mistakes. It's a formula that is very simple but incredibly effective for the Hawkeyes as they consistently rank near the top of the B1G every season in total defense.

What is most effective about this scheme is that the Hawkeyes force QB's into mistakes and they have been able to get turnovers at a high rate. This season has been on the lower side of forcing turnovers with just 9 but the fundamental aspects of the defense remain: don't miss tackles, limit big plays, force offenses to sustained drives, and don't put yourself in bad situations by blitzing too much (roughly 20% of the time).

In Iowa's recent victory over Northwestern, running back LJ Phillips racked up 128 rushing yards on 21 carries to keep control of the tempo. Purdue's defense, which has struggled to play in the backfield most of the season, must hold Iowa to minimum yards on first down to force QB Hank Brown into drop back passing situations. One aspect that has seemingly gotten better over the last several weeks on the defensive side of the ball is that they are generating some turnovers. That has got to continue by forcing Iowa into obvious passing downs where the defensive backs can worry solely about defending the pass.

Through nine games, Purdue has struggled to consistently disrupt plays in the backfield, surrendering 170.8 rushing yards per game at a 5.2-yard clip while ranking 86th nationally in stuff rate. Purdue doesn't need to suddenly become a dominant TFL team, rather, they simply need to win enough early-down reps to hold Phillips and the Hawkeye ground game to two or three yards on first down.

Doing so forces quarterback Hank Brown out of his play-action comfort zone and into pure dropback situations. While Brown has been efficient managing games this season (1,316 yards, nine touchdowns, four interceptions), Iowa isn't built to win shootouts when forced into 3rd-and-long. More importantly, winning first down unlocks what has quietly become Purdue's biggest defensive bright spot over the last month: takeaways. After recording just two turnovers through the end of September, the Boilermakers have rattled off nine more in the weeks since. When Purdue's defensive backs don't have to worry about run support or biting on play-action fakes, they can sit on routes, play aggressive coverage, and generate the kind of game-flipping turnovers required to steal a win at Kinnick.

Against Maryland, Purdue left the door wide open in the first half by settling for field goals instead of getting into the endzone. Yes, Purdue has one of the best kickers in the country with Jacobo Echeverria Lozano but Purdue really should have been leading by double digits. Instead, Purdue led just 12-7 instead of potentially 24-7 and that would have meant a very different second half.

Purdue does have the 19th rated red zone offense on the season, largely because of their freshman kicker connecting on 21-22 kicks this season. That is good for 2nd in the country and he likely has an argument for All American Honors alongside All B1G recognition. However, the Boilers have got to convert their opportunities into touchdowns if they want to beat the Hawkeyes on the road.

Iowa, at home, is too good of a football team for Purdue to settle for field goals when scoring opportunities are presented to them. Notably, Ryan Browne has got to make high level throws when the field shrinks and the throwing windows get tighter and tighter. If that isn't going to happen, Purdue better hope their offensive line is able to open up running lanes for Fame Ijeboi.

#2857. The world's super-rich are piling into alternative investments. Here's why

The world's wealthiest investors are increasingly looking beyond stocks and bonds, pouring money into hedge funds, private equity and infrastructure assets, as market volatility and fears about stubborn inflation spur diversification.

From Asian billionaires to global family offices, alternative investments are becoming a bigger part of portfolios once dominated by traditional assets, said industry watchers.

JPMorgan Asset Management's private wealth alternative assets in Asia-Pacific surged 40% over the past 12 months, according to Dan Watkins, the firm's regional chief executive officer, underscoring the growing appetite among high-net-worth and ultra-high-net-worth clients.

"So you really have this huge appetite for alternative exposure happening in that ultra-high-net-worth, high-net-worth category," Watkins said recently at a media summit in Singapore.

The shift comes as investors grapple with increasingly concentrated equity markets, geopolitical uncertainty and concerns that stocks and bonds may not provide the same protection against market downturns as they once did, experts said.

"We are seeing a sharp increase in planned asset allocation to alternatives among the family offices we speak to," said John Woods, chief investment officer for Asia at Lombard Odier.

Alternative investments now account for 60% of single-family office portfolios, surpassing traditional assets for the first time, according to BNY Wealth. Traditional assets accounted for 41% of allocations, the report found.

Wealthy investors are seeking diversification, income and access to investments outside listed markets, and concerns over elevated valuations, tight credit spreads and the growing correlation between stocks and bonds have accelerated the trend, Woods added.

Hedge funds are emerging as a favored option among wealthy investors seeking protection against market swings, particularly as traditional diversification strategies face growing challenges.

Olaolu Aganga, head of portfolio construction and analytics at Citi Wealth, said hedge funds have attracted the strongest recent interest among qualified clients, who are seeking returns less dependent on the direction of equity markets.

Private credit is another area drawing interest, offering investors contractual income streams, while some hedge fund strategies seek to profit from differences in performance between companies rather than relying on broad market rallies, said Woods.

Citi's 2026 survey of 351 family offices found that inflation was their biggest concern, followed by interest rates, financial system stability and market volatility.

Wealthy families are increasingly seeking investments that can generate income and help preserve purchasing power amid persistent inflation and interest-rate uncertainty, while diversifying portfolios against market volatility, according to Aganga.

The Citi survey, however, also showed public equities recorded the strongest net increase in allocations, suggesting wealthy investors are adding alternatives rather than abandoning traditional markets. "This is not a wholesale rotation away from public markets," Woods said.

Infrastructure has emerged as another major beneficiary of the shift, with the artificial intelligence boom creating enormous demand for data centers, electricity networks and digital connectivity.

Thomas Roland Guyot, head of private markets investment specialists for Southeast Asia at UBS Unified Global Alternatives, said investors are increasingly targeting assets that support AI, electrification and energy security.

Such investments potentially offer a combination of long-term growth and relatively predictable cash flows, particularly where revenues are supported by essential demand or long-term contracts.

Investor appetite is already showing up in fund flows. Net inflows into open-ended infrastructure funds rose 30% year on year in the 12 months through the first quarter of 2026, according to PitchBook data cited by UBS.

Private equity remains a cornerstone of wealthy investors' alternative portfolios, although they are becoming more selective. Family offices are increasingly favoring direct investments in companies, co-investments alongside private equity managers and purchases of existing fund stakes on the secondary market, rather than relying solely on traditional pooled funds, according to Citi Wealth's Aganga.

Such approaches can give investors greater control over their investments or opportunities to acquire assets at discounts, said Lombard Odier's Woods.

The broader shift is also being facilitated by the growth of evergreen and semi-liquid funds, which have lowered minimum investment thresholds and reduced some administrative hurdles associated with traditional private-market funds, Woods added.

Investing into alternative assets does not come without risks, even as mitigating those is driving flows.

Private investments can lock up capital for years, while valuations may be slow to reflect deteriorating market conditions. Even funds marketed as semi-liquid can restrict withdrawals when redemption requests surge.

"Improved access does not equal liquidity," JPMorgan's Watkins said.

Citi's Aganga also warned that performance can vary significantly between managers, while private assets may appear less volatile than they actually are because valuations are updated less frequently.

Investors must also guard against unintended concentration. A portfolio holding data centers through real estate, infrastructure and private equity funds may appear diversified while remaining heavily exposed to the same AI investment cycle, UBS' Guyot said.

For now, the flow of capital into alternative assets shows their increasing appeal among the rich, though not at the expense of traditional investment, with Woods pointing out that much of the new investment is being funded by fresh capital and excess cash rather than large-scale sales of stocks and bonds.

#2855. Jensen Huang is out and Nvidia stock is down—what market pros say about buying dips

Nvidia co-founder Jensen Huang has resigned as the firm's president and CEO, the company announced Thursday. Investors reacted negatively to the news. By 2:30 p.m., shares of the chipmaking giant were down more than 5%, and now sit 31% below their high on October 6.

It's easy to see why Huang's departure, which comes amid revelations of his romantic ties to a woman believed by the U.S. intelligence community to be a Chinese spy, has investors feeling queasy. The company Huang co-founded 33 years ago — and whose chips have powered the artificial intelligence boom — has grown into the firm currently valued at about $4 trillion under his leadership.

"The premise here is that there is no successor to a generational founder," says Michael Monaghan, portfolio manager of the Founders 100 ETF, which invests in founder-led companies. "It's that they're one of one, and what they bring from a vision, execution and grit standpoint is not replaceable."

While some investors are exiting Nvidia positions based on that premise — Monaghan's fund must, per its prospectus — others will see the slip in Nvidia's price as an opportunity to buy a great stock on proverbial sale, he says.

Looking purely at the numbers, the opportunity to buy a stock with Nvidia's prodigious projected growth at its current depressed price "happens once a decade, maybe once in a career as an investor," he says.

In investing parlance, the latter group of investors is "buying the dip" — investing in an asset after a decline in its value in anticipation of a bounceback. Whether it's a prudent strategy for you with Nvidia or any other stock requires some examination, investing experts say — not only of the stock in question, but your own portfolio strategy.

When an investment you own or are interested in declines, your first move is to examine your reason for buying or holding it, says Tom Hainlin, national investment strategist at U.S. Bank Asset Management.

Ask yourself if whatever caused the slide has changed your investment narrative, he says. "Has it changed why I bought the stock or why I'm investing in the market?"

If you own a diversified portfolio and the broad U.S. stock market has declined, buying dips is a relatively straightforward decision, investing pros say. Because the stock market has historically trended upward, each downdraft has offered investors an opportunity to add an appreciating asset at a discount.

"If you have a solid, long-term investment, it's always attractive to buy the dip because you always get a new high," Jeff Buchbinder, chief equity strategist with LPL Financial, told Make It in August. "In the history of the S&P 500, every dip has been attractive because you eventually made new highs."

Individual stocks have no such track record, meaning that a major slide should prompt you to re-examine the thesis behind any given investment, Hainlin says. That analysis is twofold. On one hand, you should rethink the position any one stock occupies in your portfolio. If it's a large enough chunk that a pullback is keeping you up at night, it might be worth paring the position back to put things more in line with your tolerance for risk, he says.

And whether you own the stock or are thinking about buying, a dip is a good time to evaluate your outlook for that investment. If you plan to hold a particular stock for the long term, for instance, you'll have to weigh whether the short-term bad news could have a lasting impact on the firm's profitability.

"Did Nvidia's prospects change as a result of [Huang's resignation]? There's no definitive answer for that," he says.

Monaghan says that, based on historical data, there may be reason to worry. His fund's fact sheet cites data from consultancy Bain & Company showing that founder-led companies vastly outperformed their peers from 1990 through 2014. Even companies that have successfully replaced their founders, such as Apple and Microsoft, have failed to grow as quickly as under original leadership, he says.

Investors will have to weigh the risk that Nvidia will lag without Huang at the helm against the firm's still impressive growth projections — the company will report third-quarter fiscal year earnings results on Nov. 17 — and their long-term belief in the firm's position as a top supplier of microchips that power AI.

"One thing we do know is that AI demand hasn't changed," Hainlin says.
