#2854. Miami's Chuck Martin slams Speedos in videoboard upright penalty rant

Miami RedHawks head coach Chuck Martin had a bizarre, lengthy and memorable reaction to the team's penalty for showing multiple uprights on the videoboard during a Mid-American Conference football win Nov. 10 against Ohio at Oxford's Yager Stadium.

With the Bobcats leading 17-7, Ohio kicker Brack Peacock attempted a field goal that hit the right upright and missed. Miami was penalized for unsportsmanlike conduct for showing multiple uprights on the videoboard behind the uprights themselves.

"On the previous play, there were depictions of the field-goal uprights on the scoreboard," the official announced just before halftime. "That is a foul for unsportsmanlike conduct against Miami. Half the distance to the goal, automatic first down."

"It was so weird, because he missed the kick," Martin told reporters Nov. 12. "There was no flag. We huddled up. We sent our offense out. There's two seconds left. We were going to take a knee, obviously. There's two seconds left. Our guys are on the field. The ball's there. There's still no flag. Now we're two minutes past the play. And there's a group of officials standing there, and I didn't think nothing of it. There's nothing going on. We're just waiting for them to put the ball in play. And the next thing I know, I see the guy just drop a flag, like literally standing there talking, and then he just puts a flag on the ground."

"I was like, what's going on?" Martin continued. "And at that point, I just thought somebody said something. One of the players on the two teams was complaining about something. Got an unsportsmanlike (flag). That's what I just figured, like, where else? And then a guy comes up to me on the sidelines, and again, it was, their job is to de-escalate the situation. Good officials de-escalate everything. Bad officials escalate everything. Doesn't matter what sport it is."

"In my wildest dream, did I think we'd be getting an unsportsmanlike conduct on something we put on a videoboard?" Martin asked. "One, I didn't even know that was possible. I know there's a rule that said, like, you can't play music on third down. ... I've seen the pictures now, because I didn't even know what was on the scoreboard. Like, they had goal posts on the scoreboard."

"I was like, 'So what?'" Martin added. "They had goal posts on the scoreboard. They had Casper the Friendly Ghost on the scoreboard. Who cared? Like, who cares what they put on the videoboard? Like, if I kicked, like, there's zero percent chance that's a distraction. Like, zero percent. I kicked. I kicked in high school and I kicked in college. I know a lot of kickers. Like, there's zero percent chance that's a distraction. Zero percent. And then I saw the picture from behind. ... Like, was he confused which goal posts they, I mean, it was, I mean, the picture from behind is ridiculous."

"Like, there's no confusion there," Martin said. "There's zero confusion. I mean, it's ridiculous. And then you put your, you take your steps, you put your head down, and then you roll. Like, it's not like you're looking at the goal posts when you're kicking, okay? Now I'm shooting a free throw, right? And there's a glass backboard that you can see through, right? And that's really where my line of sight is. Like, I can see, and there's all these students, and there's grown men, you know, college-age grown men in Speedos with shower caps on, jumping up and down in front of me, you know, in little Speedos. Like, they're not big Speedos. Like, they're really tiny Speedos. Like, when I'm not shooting free throws at basketball games or if I'm watching games on TV, it's really uncomfortable."

"It's uncomfortable that someone would come to a game and do that," Martin said. "I really appreciate it because we're trying to throw off the other team. Like, so I don't even, like, but that's not a distraction."

"There's no, underneath, like, it looks like stick figures (on the videoboard)," Martin joked. "Like, somebody looks like your fourth grader was drawing stick figures. Like, whoever came up with the graphic, isn't, like, isn't, like, on the cutting edge of, like, you know, if we were trying to distract them, we could be a little better. So, but again, it's just par for the course. It is what it is. And I don't, not anything like any, like, you saw my reaction. I was like, okay, we got to play the next play. You know, got to play the next play."

RedHawks basketball coach Travis Steele told ESPN college hoops analysts before the MAC tournament that he planned to wear a Speedo at the watch party in March. He changed his mind.

"I've gotten a bunch of (Speedos) sent to me in the mail, believe it or not," Steele had said. "I gotta see which one fits and which one's gonna flatter me the most. Listen, when Rece (Davis) put me on 'GameDay' and put me on the spot, I said, 'Listen, that's a heckuva problem to have if I have to wear a Speedo.' So I've gotta get some extra workouts and watch my diets here over the next week."

#2852. Can Warriors survive without Stephen Curry with tough stretch ahead?

On Thursday, the Golden State Warriors announced that star guard Steph Curry was diagnosed with an oblique abdominal tear and will be shelved for at least the next two weeks. The 38-year-old suffered the injury midway through the first quarter of Wednesday's 115-108 loss to the Houston Rockets.

For a team and organization that has orbited around Curry's generational gravity for nearly two decades, the loss is significant. Prior to Wednesday's loss (Curry played just five minutes), he had been averaging near-identical production to his 2016 MVP season.

The 18-year veteran has been able to shoulder a seismic load — due to the absence of Jimmy Butler and Kristaps Porziņģis' abrupt decision to step away from basketball citing health reasons — handling the ball more than ever and converting shots at the most efficient rate, all while minimizing turnovers. Curry has been the driving force for the 8-4 Warriors, who have won eight of their past nine games, which is nothing short of a testament to his greatness amid a loaded Western Conference and a team that, on paper, pales in comparison to its peers.

The Warriors will be without Stephen Curry for at least two weeks.

The Warriors' next seven games offer quite the challenge. In the coming fortnight, Golden State will face San Antonio, Dallas, Memphis, Boston and Portland, which have all won more than 60% of their games to date. But amid those upcoming hurdles, there's a strong belief within the locker room that the roster as-is can withstand Curry's absence in the short term.

"The spirit of this team is better," forward Draymond Green said to reporters following the game. "We're more certain of who we are, what makes us a good team. That puts us in a better position to be successful without him as opposed to where we were last year."

Green's assessment checks out. In 317 non-Curry minutes, the Warriors have been able to outscore teams by 14 points, according to PBP Stats, a substantial improvement from last season (Golden State lost those minutes by a whopping 135 points). The improvement of fourth-year guard Brandin Podziemski, who will now come into focus as the team's biggest perimeter threat, will be tested in the interim. Albeit in a much-smaller role alongside Curry, Podziemski has been able to clean up his shot profile and ball security, two areas of focus for him since he was drafted in 2023. This season, Podz has taken on a much larger playmaking role when Curry is off the floor and is able to draw more fouls — in addition to excelling in transition and off-ball activity.

There's also the presence of rookie Yaxel Lendeborg, a Swiss Army knife of a combo forward that is equal parts thrilling and chaotic, who has taken on enough responsibility early on and converted his chances at veteran-level clips, currently in the 87th percentile of rim success and 79th in perimeter success, according to Cleaning the Glass. The first-year phenom is the team's second-leading scorer, averaging 21.7 points per game.

If the Warriors are to survive these next two weeks, they'll need to further emphasize their unselfish identity. Without Curry's gravity, ball movement becomes even more of a premium. They're second in passes made, third in assists and first in secondary assists because of a starting lineup that possesses five players who can create with the ball in their hands to varying degrees (Lendeborg, Podziemski, Al Horford, Draymond Green and the insertion of De'Anthony Melton, who likely picks up the minutes void and is a capable creator on his own).

(Melton's presence also juices the Warriors' penchant for forcing turnovers. They're surprisingly ninth in half-court defensive efficiency, fifth in opponent turnover rate and are good at turning mistakes into points, eighth-best at the time of this writing.)

The most important factor, however, during this period of uncertainty, is the leadership structure between Green and Kerr. Those two and Curry have been the lone constants over the years, and with Curry gone, Green and Kerr should be the consistent go-tos for guidance and morale.

They share a deep understanding and longevity that transcend most partnerships in sports. And they aren't new to uncomfortable situations — whether it's internal drama, injuries, or the growing pains that come with two-timeline situations.

"I was thrilled that the guys stayed together," Kerr said after Wednesday's game. "It's a great group to coach. I really love this team and I'm excited about what's ahead."

Make no mistake, Curry's absence, even if it's for two weeks, will be felt every time this team steps on the floor. But the energy about this new group, despite the lack of outside expectations, is tangible and might just be enough to hold down the fort until 30 is ready to go again.

#2851. Here's what Nvidia interim CEO Colette Kress and board chair Harvey Jones wrote to Nvidia employees

On the heels of founder Jensen Huang's resignation as CEO of Nvidia, new interim CEO Colette Kress and board chair Harvey Jones sent emails to employees Thursday expressing gratitude and optimism for the future.

CNBC obtained copies of the emails from a source familiar with the matter, who said Jones sent the first email and Kress replied shortly after.

Follow CNBC's live coverage of Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang's resignation

"The Board, Estaff and I recognize that this has been an extraordinarily difficult period and I want you to know how grateful we are for your resilience, professionalism and continued dedication," Kress wrote in her email.

The term Estaff is commonly used to refer to executive staff, the group of direct reports to C-suite executives at a company. Nvidia will host a company meeting on November 18 at 9am PST, her email said.

Board chair Jones took a similar tone in his email, and praised Kress as "a key architect of NVIDIA's growth and capital strategy."

"The Board has full confidence in Colette to deliver on our commitments to shareholders, customers, partners, and to each of you," Jones wrote.

Below are the staff emails obtained by CNBC on Thursday.

I believe deeply in this company and in its future. More than anything, I believe in each of you, in our customers and partners and in the vital role we play every day in the global economy.

The Board, Estaff and I recognize that this has been an extraordinarily difficult period and I want you to know how grateful we are for your resilience, professionalism and continued dedication. Thank you.

This company has achieved so much because of the work that all of you bring to it every single day. You are our greatest strength. You will hear more from leadership in the coming days about our future together, and we will host a company meeting on November 18 at 9am PT.

We play a vital role in building the technology that is powering the AI revolution, and we have important work ahead of us. So let's move forward together, with the same determination and commitment that have always defined this company.

I am writing to share an important update. Jensen has resigned as CEO and Board Member, and the Board has asked our Chief Financial Officer Colette Kress to serve as interim CEO while it conducts a search for a permanent successor looking at both internal and external candidates. I will serve as the first-ever Chair of the Board.

As CFO for over 13 years, Colette has been a key architect of NVIDIA's growth and capital strategy. She possesses a deep understanding of this business and the markets in which we operate. The Board has full confidence in Colette to deliver on our commitments to shareholders, customers, partners, and to each of you.

Jensen's contributions to NVIDIA are profound. NVIDIA is one of the most consequential technology companies in the world. We pioneered accelerated computing, defined the modern GPU, and built the platform that powered artificial intelligence.

The Board takes seriously our duty as stewards of this great company.

The strength of this company resides in our employees, leadership team, technical strength and partner ecosystem. I am confident that under Colette's leadership, with the full support of the Board, we will continue to grow our business, and lead the industry forward. Thank you for your focus, your professionalism, and your commitment to NVIDIA.

Harvey JonesChair, NVIDIA Board of Directors

#2850. Top Wall Street analysts prefer these 3 dividend stocks for stable income

Global stock markets remain volatile amid persistent geopolitical tensions and concerns about the sustainability of AI trade. Given this backdrop, investors seeking steady income can add dividend-paying stocks to their portfolios.

Tracking recommendations from top Wall Street analysts can help investors pick attractive stocks, as these experts assess a company's ability to pay dividends consistently.

Here are three dividend-paying stocks that are highlighted by Wall Street's top pros, as tracked by TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Integrated oil and gas giant Chevron (CVX) is a dividend king. The company has increased its dividend for 39 consecutive years. At a quarterly dividend of $1.78 per share (annualized dividend of $7.12 per share), CVX offers a yield of 3.14%. Chevron will announce its third-quarter results on October 30.

In an earnings preview note on U.S. oil majors, Goldman Sachs analyst Neil Mehta reiterated a buy rating on Chevron with a price target of $241. The analyst is focused on the company's international growth prospects across Latin America, the Middle East, West Africa, and the Eastern Mediterranean.

Specifically, Mehta highlighted management's commentary on solid execution in Venezuela, with gross production expected to more than double to nearly 600 kbd (kilo barrels per day) by 2031 via three joint ventures. He also noted a recent oil and gas condensate discovery in offshore Angola and is awaiting commercial updates on the West Qurna 2 and Nasiriyah projects in Iraq.

Furthermore, Mehta expects CVX to report improved productivity and capital efficiency across domestic shale assets. He expects the improvement mainly in the Permian Basin, where Chevron projects advanced chemical treatments to drive about a 10% recovery uplift in new wells and bring down capital spend per barrel by about 25% year-over-year. Overall, Mehta expects Chevron to report Q3 2026 EPS of $4.95, above the consensus of $4.89.

"While unfavorable timing effects and volatile commodity prices may present quarterly earnings headwinds, we highlight healthy U.S. refining margins and strong asset utilization as partial offsets," said Mehta.

Mehta ranks No. 381 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 63% of the time, delivering an average return of 12%. See Chevron KPIs on TipRanks.

Kinetik Holdings (KNTK) is a fully integrated, pure-play, midstream company operating in the Delaware Basin. Earlier this month, the company declared a dividend of $0.81 per share, payable on October 30, 2026. With an annualized dividend of $3.24 per share, KNTK offers a yield of 5.82%. Kinetik will announce its Q3 earnings on November 4, with a conference call scheduled the next day.

Ahead of Q3 2026 earnings, Goldman Sachs analyst John Mackay reiterated a buy rating on Kinetik stock and slightly raised his price target to $58 from $57. He expects the company to report Q3 2026 EBITDA of $279 million, higher than the Street's expectation of $270 million but lower than the second quarter's EBITDA of $281 million.

The 5-star analyst expects Kinetik's Q3 performance to gain from higher gas volumes after notable curtailments in the second quarter. Mackay also expects the quarter to benefit from higher production and steady commodity margins, even though some lower-margin contracts and weaker Waha spread marketing gains could weigh on results.

Overall, Mackay increased his Q3 2026 estimates to reflect higher volumes, driven by rapid Permian egress pipeline commissioning, stronger throughput at Kings Landing I, and higher M2M commodity prices, offset by increased costs.

"As natural gas takeaway constraints ease across the Permian, investor focus centers on KNTK's YE26 volume trajectory and durability of its multi-year growth," said Mackay.

Mackay ranks No. 569 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 64% of the time, delivering an average return of 16.9%. See Kinetik's Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

Moving to Crescent Energy (CRGY), an exploration and production company with activities focused in the Eagle Ford, Permian, and Uinta Basins. With a quarterly dividend of $0.12 per share (annualized dividend of $0.48 per share), CRGY's dividend yield stands at 3.56%. Earlier this month, Crescent announced an agreement to acquire Eagle Ford assets from Devon Energy (DVN).

Heading into Q3 earnings on Nov. 2, Evercore analyst Stephen Richardson reiterated a buy rating on Crescent stock and slightly lowered the price target to $17 from $18, citing dilution from new shares.

"CRGY's recent acquisition of DVN's Eagle Ford assets marks a strategic, growth-oriented move, potentially enhancing cash flow performance," said Richardson.

The 5-star analyst noted that the acquisition of the Eagle Ford assets at a net purchase price of $3.85 billion will add about 68 MBoe/d of net production and over 600 net Tier 1 locations. Richardson added that a solid track record of synergies from past deals in the Eagle Ford region increases confidence about Crescent achieving efficiencies with this deal.

Additionally, Richardson noted that the financial implications of the acquisition seem impressive, with a projected increase in cash flow per share of about 9% for 2027 to 2029.

The analyst believes that Crescent is not just expanding its business through acquisitions but positioning itself as a more durable company, regardless of market conditions.

Richardson ranks No. 478 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 63% of the time, delivering an average return of 13.2%. See Crescent Energy Statistics on TipRanks.

#2849. Nvidia: Ex-prosecutor Joon Kim and Wachtell, Lipton bring big track record to investigation

Joon Kim, the lawyer tapped by artificial intelligence chip giant Nvidia to investigate former CEO Jensen Huang, and the Big Law firm he works for have a long history of investigating high-profile cases.

Kim, in his career as a top federal prosecutor and as an attorney in private practice, has had in his sights mobster John "The Teflon Don" Gotti's son, former New York Gov. Andrew Cuomo, the Federal Deposit Insurance Corp. and other high-profile people and companies.

The law firm that Kim recently joined — Wachtell, Lipton, Rosen & Katz — this year conducted an investigation for the NBA of the Los Angeles Clippers and its billionaire owner, Steve Ballmer, which ended with the basketball league imposing stiff sanctions on Ballmer and the Clippers for violating salary cap rules in connection with superstar Kawhi Leonard.

On Tuesday, Nvidia said it had retained Kim, 55, and Wachtell, Lipton to "conduct a thorough and independent investigation" of allegations that its then-CEO Jensen Huang had been the target of a foreign intelligence effort involving his affair with a woman, Alice Huang. U.S. intelligence suspects her of being a spy for China.

Jensen Huang resigned as CEO on Thursday morning on the heels of a New York Times report that he had also had an extramarital affair with an Nvidia executive, Kimberly Powell, who reports to him.

That allegation could be added to the list of issues that Kim will be looking into as part of his probe.

Nvidia did not immediately respond when asked if the parameters of the investigation would be affected by the new claim. A spokeswoman for Wachtell, Lipton declined to comment for this article on behalf of the firm and Kim.

Friends and former colleagues of Kim said Nvidia has made a smart decision in retaining him for the job.

"I think he's the ideal person for this role," said Edward Kim, a New York white-collar defense lawyer who worked in the U.S. Attorney's Office for the Southern District of New York with Joon Kim. The two men are not related.

"I think they're going to get a serious, credible investigation. I believe Joon will follow the facts wherever they will lead," Edward Kim said.

The son of a South Korean diplomat, Joon Kim was born in Los Angeles. He attended Phillips Exeter Academy and later Stanford University.

After graduating cum laude from Harvard Law School, he clerked for a federal judge in Manhattan and then worked as an associate at the major law firm Cleary Gottlieb Steen & Hamilton starting in the late 1990s. After that, he spent seven years in the SDNY as an assistant U.S. attorney. His tenure as a prosecutor there included four years in the Organized Crime and Terrorism Unit.

The SDNY, by virtue of its location in Manhattan, handles some of the most high-profile criminal investigations in the United States, including cases of major financial crimes, government corruption and organized crime.

During his first stint at the SDNY, Kim investigated and prosecuted crimes that included "racketeering, murder, money laundering, securities fraud, firearms and narcotics offenses, tax evasion, and terrorism," the office has previously said.

Kim's trial wins at the SDNY included obtaining a conviction of Peter Gotti, then-head of the Gambino Mafia family, for conspiring to kill notorious mob turncoat Salvatore "Sammy the Bull" Gravano. Kim and his fellow prosecutors were less successful, however, in prosecuting Peter Gotti's nephew, John Gotti Jr., whose trials ended with several hung juries.

"He could be a very tenacious courtroom advocate," said Chris Conniff, a New York attorney who worked with Kim in the SDNY, and who considers Kim one of his closest friends.

"He's incredibly smart," Conniff said. "I think he's known by everyone in the business as just an outstanding investigator, leaves no stone unturned."

Kim in 2006 returned to Cleary Gottlieb as a partner in the firm's litigation and enforcement group but then went back to the SDNY in 2013. During his second stint there, he served as deputy U.S. attorney, and then served as acting U.S. attorney from 2017 to 2018.

Kim's elevation to acting U.S. attorney came as a result of President Donald Trump's firing of then-U.S. Attorney Preet Bharara in March 2017 after Bharara refused the new president's demand that he resign.

"Preet's departure was very unique," and the mood in the SDNY "was shaky" after his ouster, Conniff said.

"Preet was very well-liked and well-respected," said Conniff, who was in private practice at the time that Bharara was fired. "We just lost a very charismatic and effective leader, and in a very dramatic way."

In taking over from Bharara, Kim, "from my external viewpoint, he righted the ship and continued to do the work of the office in the time he was there."

"Joon, I would say, gave people confirmation that it was going to be alright, and it was going to be business as usual," Conniff said.

Kim left the SDNY in April 2018, and began his third stint at Cleary Gottlieb, which he recently left to join Wachtell, Lipton's litigation department.

In March 2021, Kim and another attorney, Anne Clark, a partner at Vladeck, Raskin & Clark, were tapped by New York Attorney General Letitia James to investigate then-Gov. Andrew Cuomo over allegations that he had sexually harassed several women.

"These are serious allegations that demand a rigorous and impartial investigation," Kim said in a statement at the time. "We will act judiciously and follow the facts wherever they lead."

Kim and Clark in August 2021 submitted a 165-page report to James, which concluded that Cuomo had sexually harassed at least 11 women and then retaliated against a former employee who had publicly complained about his behavior. As part of the probe, Kim, Clark and their team interviewed 179 witnesses and reviewed tens of thousands of documents.

Cuomo adamantly denied some of the allegations and said that other claims of alleged misconduct had been mischaracterized or misinterpreted.

A week after the report was publicly released, Cuomo announced that he would resign.

Two years after the Cuomo probe, Kim was retained by the FDIC to conduct an independent review of allegations of sexual harassment and interpersonal misconduct at the agency. As part of the probe, Kim and his team conducted 167 interviews of current and past FDIC employees, and hundreds of additional interviews with individuals who contacted a hotline the team set up for employees to report misconduct claims.

An April 2024 report by Kim and his team found "for far too many employees and for far too long, the FDIC has failed to provide a workplace safe from sexual harassment, discrimination, and other interpersonal misconduct."

More than five years after Cuomo left office, the former governor's spokesman has not forgotten, or forgiven, Kim, whom he accused of writing a report on Cuomo with a predetermined outcome.

Kim was a "hired hack," said the spokesman, Rich Azzopardi.

Azzopardi said Kim "hid evidence" that was exonerating of Cuomo, and accepted at face value "outright lies" from a number of witnesses.

The bad blood between Cuomo's camp and Kim predates the sexual harassment probe.

While working in the SDNY, Kim and Bharara oversaw the investigation that led to the corruption trial of Joseph Percoco, who had been a close aide of Cuomo. The trial took place after both prosecutors had left the office.

Azzopardi cited that prosecution in his scathing remarks about Kim.

Percoco was sentenced to six years in prison after his 2018 conviction for honest-services wire fraud. The conviction was overturned by the U.S. Supreme Court in a unanimous ruling in 2023 — after his release from prison — because the judge's instructions to jurors were too vague.

Azzopardi said that Kim and Bharara were motivated to prosecute Percoco for "their own personal glory" and "to generate headlines for themselves."

Bharara, in a two-word email response about Azzopardi's claim, wrote, "Andrew who?," referring to Cuomo.

Azzopardi said he did not believe there was any other reason for Nvidia's board to retain Kim to investigate the Huang situation other than to reach a predetermined outcome by the board.

"I don't know what the aims of the board are," Azzopardi said.

Kim's friend Conniff, when asked if he expected that the Nvidia investigation would be a whitewash that would serve the board's interests, said, "No."

"I don't think Joon would take an engagement in which he could not provide a fulsome report of what he uncovered during this investigation," Conniff said. "Any company board that is looking to do a good, thorough investigation of potential wrongdoing, Joon's perfect ... for it."

#2848. Miami's Chuck Martin slams Speedos in videoboard upright penalty rant

Miami RedHawks head coach Chuck Martin had a bizarre, lengthy and memorable reaction to the team's penalty for showing multiple uprights on the videoboard during a Mid-American Conference football win Nov. 10 against Ohio at Oxford's Yager Stadium.

With the Bobcats leading 17-7, Ohio kicker Brack Peacock attempted a field goal that hit the right upright and missed. Miami was penalized for unsportsmanlike conduct for showing multiple uprights on the videoboard behind the uprights themselves.

"On the previous play, there were depictions of the field-goal uprights on the scoreboard," the official announced just before halftime. "That is a foul for unsportsmanlike conduct against Miami. Half the distance to the goal, automatic first down."

"It was so weird, because he missed the kick," Martin told reporters Nov. 12. "There was no flag. We huddled up. We sent our offense out. There's two seconds left. We were going to take a knee, obviously. There's two seconds left. Our guys are on the field. The ball's there. There's still no flag. Now we're two minutes past the play. And there's a group of officials standing there, and I didn't think nothing of it. There's nothing going on. We're just waiting for them to put the ball in play. And the next thing I know, I see the guy just drop a flag, like literally standing there talking, and then he just puts a flag on the ground."

"I was like, what's going on?" Martin continued. "And at that point, I just thought somebody said something. One of the players on the two teams was complaining about something. Got an unsportsmanlike (flag). That's what I just figured, like, where else? And then a guy comes up to me on the sidelines, and again, it was, their job is to de-escalate the situation. Good officials de-escalate everything. Bad officials escalate everything. Doesn't matter what sport it is."

"In my wildest dream, did I think we'd be getting an unsportsmanlike conduct on something we put on a videoboard?" Martin asked. "One, I didn't even know that was possible. I know there's a rule that said, like, you can't play music on third down. ... I've seen the pictures now, because I didn't even know what was on the scoreboard. Like, they had goal posts on the scoreboard."

"I was like, 'So what?'" Martin added. "They had goal posts on the scoreboard. They had Casper the Friendly Ghost on the scoreboard. Who cared? Like, who cares what they put on the videoboard? Like, if I kicked, like, there's zero percent chance that's a distraction. Like, zero percent. I kicked. I kicked in high school and I kicked in college. I know a lot of kickers. Like, there's zero percent chance that's a distraction. Zero percent. And then I saw the picture from behind. ... Like, was he confused which goal posts they, I mean, it was, I mean, the picture from behind is ridiculous."

"Like, there's no confusion there," Martin said. "There's zero confusion. I mean, it's ridiculous. And then you put your, you take your steps, you put your head down, and then you roll. Like, it's not like you're looking at the goal posts when you're kicking, okay? Now I'm shooting a free throw, right? And there's a glass backboard that you can see through, right? And that's really where my line of sight is. Like, I can see, and there's all these students, and there's grown men, you know, college-age grown men in Speedos with shower caps on, jumping up and down in front of me, you know, in little Speedos. Like, they're not big Speedos. Like, they're really tiny Speedos. Like, when I'm not shooting free throws at basketball games or if I'm watching games on TV, it's really uncomfortable."

"It's uncomfortable that someone would come to a game and do that," Martin said. "I really appreciate it because we're trying to throw off the other team. Like, so I don't even, like, but that's not a distraction."

"There's no, underneath, like, it looks like stick figures (on the videoboard)," Martin joked. "Like, somebody looks like your fourth grader was drawing stick figures. Like, whoever came up with the graphic, isn't, like, isn't, like, on the cutting edge of, like, you know, if we were trying to distract them, we could be a little better. So, but again, it's just par for the course. It is what it is. And I don't, not anything like any, like, you saw my reaction. I was like, okay, we got to play the next play. You know, got to play the next play."

RedHawks basketball coach Travis Steele told ESPN college hoops analysts before the MAC tournament that he planned to wear a Speedo at the watch party in March. He changed his mind.

"I've gotten a bunch of (Speedos) sent to me in the mail, believe it or not," Steele had said. "I gotta see which one fits and which one's gonna flatter me the most. Listen, when Rece (Davis) put me on 'GameDay' and put me on the spot, I said, 'Listen, that's a heckuva problem to have if I have to wear a Speedo.' So I've gotta get some extra workouts and watch my diets here over the next week."

Konata Mumpfield, Tutu Atwell, Xavier Smith and rookie CJ Daniels are other receivers on the roster.

#2846. Joel Embiid scores 20 in 76ers' win over Raptors after knee injury scare; LeBron James sat out for back management

After being listed as questionable up until game time, Joel Embiid scored 20 points in the Philadelphia 76ers' 123-112 win over the Toronto Raptors on Thursday.

Embiid also avoided what initially appeared to be a knee injury early in the first quarter when Ja'Kobe Walter ran into him trying to avoid a screen. Walter's right knee collided with Embiid's right knee, sending the center to the floor. Embiid pointed to his knee as trainers came out to check him, causing fears that he may have been seriously hurt.

However, Embiid re-entered the game three minutes later and scored his first basket with under two minutes remaining in the first quarter.

Up until a half-hour before tip-off, Embiid was listed on the injury report as questionable with "right foot plantar fascia injury management." That had also sidelined him for two of the Sixers' game in the early season.

"I have no idea," Embiid said when asked by ESPN's Kris Rhim during shootaround if he was playing versus the Raptors. "We'll see what they have to say and then we'll go from there."

Tyrese Maxey scored 27 to lead the Sixers, also dishing out 8 assists. Jaylen Brown added 18 with 6 rebounds, Anfernee Simons contributed 17 points (hitting 5-of-6 3-pointers) off the bench and VJ Edgecombe finished with 15 points, 8 board, 8 assists and 3 steals. Philadelphia improved its record to 9-3, 5-2 at home.

For the Raptors, RJ Barrett led all scorers with 31 points (shooting 4-for-7 on 3s) in addition to 14 rebounds and 5 assists. Immanuel Quickley added 26 points, 4 rebounds and 6 assists. Collin Murray-Boyles scored 14 points with 4 assists and 5 blocks. Toronto dropped to 5-7 (3-4 on the road) with the defeat.

While Embiid played, LeBron James sat out his third game of the early 2026-27 season on Thursday. James was listed on the Sixers' injury report as out with "low back/sciatica management," which had been the reason he's sat two of Philadelphia's previous 12 games this season.

The 41-year-old was listed as questionable until participating in Thursday's shootaround. He was then downgraded.

Thursday's game was not part of a back-to-back for the 76ers, so that was not a consideration in sitting James and possibly resting Embiid. Philadelphia last played on Tuesday, losing 123-101 to the Boston Celtics in the hyped first meeting between former teammates Jaylen Brown and Jayson Tatum.

However, the Sixers do play back-to-back games on Sunday and Monday, hosting the Indiana Pacers then visiting the Brooklyn Nets.

So far this season, James is averaging 15.1 points, 7.2 rebounds and 6.5 assists on 43% shooting from the floor. Embiid is scoring 17.7 points per game with 9.3 rebounds and 3.8 assists. He is also shooting 37%, while making only 4 of 33 (12%) 3-point attempts.

Without Embiid, Philadelphia would have been short-handed at center with Adem Bona (cervical spine sprain) and Dominick Barlow (low back stress reaction) also out for Thursday. Perhaps that influenced the decision to play him against the Raptors.

James started at center when Embiid was sidelined on Oct. 25 versus the Detroit Pistons. Had Embiid been out, the Sixers would have faced going small with 6-foot-7 Jabari Walker in the middle (who subbed in when Embiid left the game), or perhaps played 7-foot-6 Tacko Fall for only the second time this season. Fall played one minute in Thursday's win.

#2845. Cerebras surges 17% after beating on quarterly revenue and announcing Microsoft deal

Cerebras Systems reported better-than-expected results and quarterly guidance in its earnings report on Thursday, and touted a new deal with Microsoft. The stock soared 17% in extended trading.

Here's how the company performed relative to LSEG consensus:

Cerebras' core revenue grew 70% from $135.7 million, compared with 103% growth in the second quarter, according to a statement.

"I think there is opportunity created by, particularly on the hardware side, the sale of hardware, where long lead times in GPUs and more expense creates opportunity for us," Cerebras CEO Andrew Feldman told CNBC in an interview, referring to graphics processing units.

Executives will announce a new relationship with Microsoft, one of the world's top cloud providers, on a conference call with analysts, Feldman said. The call starts at 5 p.m. ET.

"We believe revenue begins to show up in Q1 '27," Feldman said in the interview, adding that Microsoft signed the deal in October. Cerebras has also landed deals with other major cloud providers Amazon and Oracle.

Cerebras is evaluating its financing options to meet the ramping demand, Feldman said in the interview.

The company is trying to challenge Nvidia in the AI chip market. Its earnings land on the same day that Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang resigned from the company he co-founded after multiple scandals involving extramarital affairs.

For the fourth quarter, Cerebras expects a core gross margin of 47% to 49%, with $310 million to $330 million in revenue. Analysts polled by LSEG were looking for a 42.1% margin and $271 million in revenue.

Cerebras said it expects revenue to triple in 2027. That implies about $2.85 billion in revenue. Analysts surveyed by LSEG were looking for $2.92 billion.

Cerebras shares have slumped by about 47% since the company debuted on Nasdaq in May.

#2843. Sports Media Loves Second Chances Until Dianna Russini Needs One

Apparently, Dianna Russini didn't apologize enough. Didn't explain enough and didn't show enough remorse to satisfy the sports media's newly appointed Department of Moral Superiority. So, now what?

Do we revoke her press credentials for life? Force her to stand before 300 sportswriters and beg for forgiveness before reporting a rumored trade? The reaction to Russini's return has been something to behold.

After seven months away, she returned with a new show and column for BetUS. Russini discussing her family, the difficult months away and her excitement about covering football again. What she didn't do was directly address the controversy involving Patriots coach Mike Vrabel.

The media wanted answers, accountability and perhaps a televised confession followed by a walk of shame through the NFL owners' meetings. Who appointed us judge, jury and executioner?

Let's establish something immediately. Dianna Russini crossed a serious line. The Athletic investigated her relationship with Vrabel after photographs surfaced of them together at an Arizona resort. Its review concluded that the relationship violated company standards and should have been disclosed to editors. Regardless of whether it was romantic or not.

Journalism depends on trust. If editors can't trust you to disclose potential conflicts, why should audiences trust your reporting?

Here's where the story gets interesting. The Athletic reviewed hundreds of articles, videos and podcast appearances. It found no inaccuracies in Russini's work involving Vrabel, although it criticized some coverage as overly flattering and recommended editors' notes. That's an important distinction. Violating professional standards doesn't automatically mean every story you've reported was dishonest. Also, it certainly doesn't mean you should never work again.

Now, let's discuss something our industry conveniently avoids. The NFL insider business is all about relationships. Not just professional handshakes and exchanging Christmas cards. We're talking about reporters spending years developing close relationships with players, coaches, agents and front-office executives.

They text at midnight, share meals, protect sources and sometimes become friends with the very people they're supposed to cover objectively.

It's an uncomfortable dance. Insiders need information, while sources want favorable access, influence or simply someone willing to tell their side. Everybody wants something. The closer those relationships become, the harder it can be to distinguish independent reporting from delivering somebody else's message.

Let's not pretend Russini invented this problem.

Plenty of reporters are awfully cozy with the people they cover. Some practically have reserved parking spaces outside NFL general managers' offices. Others deliver information so favorable to a particular organization that you wonder whether the press release came with a byline.

Does that make every insider unethical? Of course not. Relationships are how this business works, but they're also where its biggest ethical dangers begin. Russini took it too far. The larger issue isn't whether reporters develop relationships. It's whether they maintain enough professional distance to tell the truth when those relationships become inconvenient.

That's the standard everyone should face, including the people currently throwing stones.

Sports media is filled with enormous egos, questionable conflicts and people who've received second, third and occasionally fourth chances. Some of these glass houses have floor-to-ceiling windows, and the people inside are throwing bricks.

There's another uncomfortable reality here.

Women in sports media have spent decades fighting unfair assumptions about how they obtain access and information. Russini's decisions gave people who already embrace those stereotypes another excuse to question women doing outstanding work. She should be held accountable because she's a journalist, not because she's a woman. However, she should also recognize that her decisions affected more than her own reputation.

Now, can we remember who matters in this business? The fans. Remember them? The people who watch, listen, read and ultimately determine whether any of us has a job?

Here's a bold prediction. If Russini breaks a blockbuster NFL story tomorrow, fans will read it. If she reports that a franchise quarterback is being traded, nobody's refusing to click because her apology didn't satisfy the sports media ethics committee. They're going to want to know who's getting traded and whether their team gave up three first-round picks to acquire him.

Fans want information, accuracy, access and news before everybody else has it. That's the business. Credibility matters enormously. If Russini starts getting stories wrong, demonstrates favoritism or becomes nothing more than a mouthpiece for sources, she has a serious problem.

If she consistently delivers accurate, independent reporting, shouldn't that count?

I'm also curious about her new home. BetUS is an offshore sportsbook, and the relationship between gambling companies and journalists deserves scrutiny. Information moves betting markets, making editorial independence and transparency especially important. Russini should expect more scrutiny than most reporters because of what happened.

She earned that, but scrutiny isn't the same as rooting for failure.

We celebrate redemption stories in sports constantly. Players screw up, coaches screw up and executives screw up. Yet we preach accountability, growth and second chances. Then someone in our industry makes a mistake, and suddenly everybody's auditioning for district attorney.

Besides, would another apology really satisfy the critics? A prepared statement would be called scripted. Tears would be dismissed as playing the victim. A detailed explanation would generate demands for even more details. There's no satisfying a jury that's already reached its verdict.

Russini lost a prominent job. Her reputation took a beating, and her personal life became national entertainment. Those are real consequences. She owes future employers honesty, audiences transparency and accurate reporting. But also herself a better understanding of professional boundaries.

What she doesn't owe is a lifetime of apologies to every sports media personality with a podcast microphone.

Maybe she'll rebuild her credibility, and maybe she won't. That depends on what she reports, how she reports it and whether people believe her. We're not here to decide who gets into heaven; we're here to inform and entertain an audience.

Dianna Russini doesn't need the sports media's forgiveness. She needs the fans' trust. If she earns it back, the audience will decide her future, not the people throwing stones from inside their own glass houses.

The post Sports Media Loves Second Chances Until Dianna Russini Needs One appeared first on Barrett Media.

#2841. Trump says ‘we don’t need a deal with Iran’

The U.S. should no longer seek a deal with Iran, President Donald Trump said Thursday as he directed Vice President JD Vance to instead focus his attention on corruption and crime in Democratic cities and states.

"We don't need a deal with Iran. Those days have long since passed," Trump wrote in a Truth Social post.

"We've got their money, Hundreds of Billions of Dollars, and we've got their Strait!" Trump wrote, referring to the Strait of Hormuz, a key choke point in the ongoing U.S.-Iran war.

The announcement came just over a week after Republicans suffered huge losses in the midterm election. Vance and other Republicans have pinned those losses on the Iran war, which the U.S. and Israel started in late February.

In the weeks leading up to Election Day, Trump repeatedly suggested the U.S. could resume launching military strikes, which have largely subsided as a tense stalemate has taken hold in the region.

The Wall Street Journal on Wednesday reported that Vance, who has led diplomatic efforts with Iran in prior months, was considering face-to-face talks with a top Iranian security official.

Stock futures declined following Trump's post.

The unpopular Iran war, which Trump and others in his administration said would be won in a matter of weeks, is now in its ninth month with no clear end in sight. The conflict has driven up global energy prices, creating a ripple effect through the economy and exacerbating concerns about inflation and affordability that were paramount to voters in the midterms.

Corruption emerged as another top issue that broke in Democrats' favor in the election. Trump's post Thursday morning attempts to flip the script, blaming Democratic-run areas for allowing corruption and crime to proliferate.

"I have asked Vice President Vance to focus his complete and total attention on the Massive Corruption and Crime taking place in Democrat run Cities and States throughout the U.S.," Trump wrote.

"The Blue States, such as Minnesota, California, Illinois, and many more, are all poorly run, corrupt disasters. Billions of Dollars is being stolen each and every month," the president wrote. "Our Budget could be quickly BALANCED with the correction of this large scale criminal activity."

"CORRUPTION is in the Democrat Leadership's blood!" he added.

#2840. Robinhood shares fall as crypto slump weighs on profits, offsetting gains in new businesses

Robinhood shares were down around 8% in pre-market trading on Wednesday as investors digested weaker-than-expected third-quarter earnings despite growth in options trading and prediction markets.

Here's how the brokerage performed in its quarter ended Sept. 30, compared with Wall Street estimates, based on a survey of analysts by LSEG:

Robinhood's net income fell to $468 million, or 51 cents per share, from $556 million, or 61 cents per share, a year ago. Revenue represented a 12% increase from $1.27 billion in the same quarter last year.

Despite that gain, transaction-based revenue of $867 million came in below Wall Street estimates from StreetAccount of $879 million, putting focus on whether Robinhood can sustain momentum across its trading businesses and reduce its reliance on volatile market activity.

Robinhood has been diversifying its business as it tries to expand its brokerage app into a broader financial services platform. Options trading and event contracts have become increasingly important sources of transaction revenue, while the company has continued to invest in wealth management, subscriptions and other offerings.

Transaction-based revenue grew 19% from the same period last year to $867 million, driven primarily by a more than 500% increase in event contracts revenue. Additionally, options revenue grew 31% to $399 million and equities revenue rose 41% to $121 million.

Those gains were partially offset by cryptocurrency revenue, which fell 55% to $120 million.

Chief Financial Officer Shiv Verma told CNBC event contracts on crypto movements did well in the quarter, offering investors another way to express a view on crypto despite the slump in prices and spot trading.

Net deposits on the Robinhood platform totaled $15.7 billion for the quarter, equal to a 17% annualized growth rate based on total platform assets at the end of Q2 2026. Over the past year, net deposits totaled $71 billion, representing 21% growth compared with total platform assets at the end of Q3 2025.

—With reporting from CNBC's Robert Hum and Kate Rooney

#2839. Seahawks-Raiders Thursday injury update: Charles Cross gets back to (limited) work!

There's one more week to go before a much-needed bye week for the Seattle Seahawks with a matchup against the Las Vegas Raiders on the horizon. The injury report is still quite hefty with 15 players listed for Seattle compared to just 4 for the Raiders.

However, there was a positive turn as Charles Cross was a limited participant in Thursday's practice after missing the last two games. In all likelihood, Seattle won't rush Cross back and will still trot out Josh Jones at LT in Sin City. It's a good sign for Cross to potentially return after the bye week if he's already taking some reps now.

Rashid Shaheed was a full participant for the second day in a row. Barring a setback, it sure looks like Shaheed will be able to play on Sunday, adding some much needed speed and explosiveness back on the field.

Other than Jaxon Smith-Njigba returning to practice after a rest day, all of Wednesday's practice participations were the same. That means limited participation for pretty much everyone except for Sam Darnold, Quenton Nelson, and Leonard Williams.

Jarran Reed missing practice for a personal matter for the last two days is slightly concerning. Seattle will need him back on the field with Big Cat still on the mend.

On the other side of the Raiders game is the all-important bye week. Hopefully the injury list will get much shorter after that.

#2838. Ball State women's basketball falls to No. 7 Louisville in encouraging outing

MUNCIE — Nearly 13 years ago, on Dec. 18, 2013, Ball State women's basketball hosted No. 7 Louisville, marking the first time ever that the Cardinals hosted a top-10 team in Worthen Arena. Ball State lost that game by 38 points.

On Thursday, Nov. 12, 2026, Ball State women's basketball hosted a top-10 team for the second time in program history. Coincidentally, it was Louisville again, and in an even bigger coincidence, the visiting Cardinals were ranked No. 7 again.

Ball State fared much better than it did 13 years ago, but ultimately couldn't keep up with its elite competition, falling to Louisville in a 94-73 result.

Despite the 21-point deficit on the scoreboard at the end of the night, Ball State head coach Brady Sallee was very pleased with his team's performance and approach to the game following a tough loss to High Point in their last game.

"How much fun was that?" Sallee said. "I'm telling you, those are the ones I like to play. Man, they're good, but I told this group, I think we were good too. I can get down with that team that I just watched play. That team last Saturday, I was about ready to take them back to Enterprise Rent-A-Car and trade it out. But this group, man, I can get down with that. I think we got so much better in the last three or four days."

More: Ball State women's basketball falls to High Point in mid-major battle

Ball State's shortcomings in this loss were obvious — the home Cardinals were physically overmatched by the visitors. After all, Louisville is a top-10 team for a reason, and they looked every bit the part of a Final Four contender in their win at Worthen Arena.

The visiting Cardinals used their size and length to fluster Ball State's offense, finishing with 12 steals and seven blocks as a team. Many of their blocks came on Ball State 3-point attempts where Louisville's players barely had to jump.

"They're very athletic," Ball State freshman Lilli Barnes said. "They're big, they're tall, they're lengthy, so they're very active on defense and can read everything."

In spite of the physical mismatch, Ball State's players never looked like they were intimidated by the competition. Much like Sallee, they embraced the opportunity to compete with an elite opponent and see how they measure up against a program that has become a Sweet 16 regular in the 2020s. That fearless mentality began in practice, where the Cardinals had a much better week after Sallee called out their preparation following their loss to High Point.

"I think it's just the mindset we go into practice with," junior Grace Kingery said. "We have to take everything seriously, especially when we go through scout and knowing the personnel and stuff, so I think it's just being locked in, especially at shootaround too."

Barnes made her first collegiate start and led Ball State with 22 points on 7-of-10 field goal shooting while also pulling down a team-high seven rebounds. The heralded freshman out of Valparaiso has wasted no time in establishing herself as a rising star for Ball State, and it's become clear through three games that she will have a major impact on the Cardinals' success this season. Achieving that status often comes with a lot of pressure, especially for such a young player, but Barnes is built for the moment.

"I think before every game, there's a little bit of nerves whether you're starting or just on the bench," Barnes said. "Once the ball goes up, just playing and getting a feel for the game, I've been playing all my life, so it just comes as the game goes on."

Zhen Verburgt finished second on the team in scoring with 13 points, while Kingery followed with 12 points.

Ball State benefitted from a large and loud home crowd, particularly a very full and raucous student section. Sallee was very complimentary of the student section's energy throughout the game, and the players agreed that it played a part in their own energetic performance.

"It helped a lot," Kingery said. "It was so much fun, especially when we cut the lead down to within 10. It was great."

Sallee professed postgame that he is not a fan of moral victories and is more concerned with his team's process of getting better. This week, he felt that their process was sound and that the team was ready to play against an elite Louisville team. Although the final score was lopsided against them, it was a good night for Ball State women's basketball.

"I think it's important that, in your own locker room, you define success for yourself," Sallee said. "You don't necessarily chase wins and losses — those become byproducts of doing everything right. For us, we work and we walk about it a lot, about what success means to us. We could play Ave Maria Technical State College of the who-knows-what and win, but I don't know that, if your process isn't good, would you be happy with that win? It's just fool's gold."

Ball State's next opponent will be much tougher than the fictional Ave Maria Tech State, as the Cardinals are set to host yet another ranked team when No. 21 Illinois comes to Muncie on Wednesday, Nov. 18. The Cardinals and Fighting Illini will tip off at 6:30 p.m. ET at Worthen Arena.

#2837. Sandro Mamukelashvili threw a jab at the Bucks after helping Lakers to win

Years ago, Sandro Mamukelashvili began his career as a member of the Milwaukee Bucks, where he spent his first 1.5 NBA seasons after they drafted him 54th overall in 2021. But in March of 2023, they waived him before he joined the San Antonio Spurs for 2.5 years then ultimately established himself with the Toronto Raptors in 2025-26.

He parlayed that breakout campaign into a four-year, $52 million deal with the Los Angeles Lakers this past summer and has been absolutely sensational to begin the season. On Wednesday against the Bucks, he authored one of his best games thus far with 23 points (9-of-15 shooting, 5-of-8 from deep), three rebounds, two assists and one steal. In the win, Los Angeles outscored Milwaukee by 21 points during his 26 minutes.

Following the victory — the Lakers' seventh in a row — Mamukelashvili didn't pass up the opportunity to point out what Milwaukee lost when it parted ways with him years ago.

"I'm happy we got this dub," he said postgame. "Even though I got drafted here, it's always great to come back and beat the team that waived you."

Mamukelashvili has been among the league's top reserves this season, elevating the Lakers' offense with his shooting, passing, driving and transition ball-handling. They deploy him in a variety of lineups, where he spends time at both power forward and center. According to databallr, they're scoring 128.1 points per 100 possessions when he's on the floor.

Regardless of whether he's sharing the court with Walker Kessler, the Lakers are thriving in his minutes, which allows them to slot him among the starters or help anchor bench-heavy units — the result of his size and wide-ranging offensive skill-set.

Los Angeles gets a few days off before concluding its road trip Sunday evening against the Phoenix Suns at 7 p.m., when it aims for an eighth straight win. Averaging 23 points, 5.5 rebounds, 2.5 assists and 1.0 steals over his past two outings, Mamukelashvili will hope to stay in a groove himself and keep helping the 11-2 Lakers stack victories.

#2836. How early-season shot selection shifts are impacting fantasy basketball value

We're far enough into the season to notice something useful, as long as we're careful about what we're measuring. Scoring averages and shooting percentages can swing a lot over the first few weeks. Where a player takes his shots tends to settle faster, so it's a better place to start.

In testing this for 2025-26, Tall Order Digital found that among 156 qualifying players, shot-zone shares over the first four games correlated with the rest of the season at 0.72 to 0.86 (a perfect match would be 1.0). Field-goal percentage correlated at just 0.46.

To see what the shifts are doing to fantasy basketball value, I've listed each player's preseason ADP next to his current 9-cat rank (through Nov. 11). Shot data is Basketball-Reference totals as of Nov. 11, compared with last season or a career mark.

Luka Dončić and De'Aaron Fox are ranked higher than where they were drafted, and Michael Porter Jr. has climbed despite scoring less. In all three cases, the revised shot profiles are landing and netting positive results.

He's launching 13.4 3s a game, up from 10.8 last season (which led the NBA). His 3-point share has risen from .476 to .549, and last year's .476 was already a career high. The bigger move is at the rim, where his share has dropped from .076 to .060. He's making 40.8% of his 3s and averaging 38.6 points in 11 games.

Fox is getting to the line far more often. He's attempted 53 free throws on 111 field-goal attempts, a free-throw rate of .477, compared with .235 last season and a .316 career mark. His career high is .420, set in 2019-20, so this is a new peak.

It isn't coming from more shots at the rim, though. His rim share is flat (.207 vs .201). What changed is the middle of the floor. Shots from 3-10 feet jumped from .204 to .315 of his attempts, while long twos nearly vanished (.067 to .018). He's also attempting 3.1 3s a game, down from 5.5, so his average shot is 12.4 feet from the basket instead of 14.4.

He's traded long twos and 3s for more floaters and short jumpers, and the whistle is doing the rest. He's backing it up with 51% shooting and 6.2 assists, though his scoring is 14.2 points, down from 18.6.

Porter is taking 3s on .615 of his attempts, up from .506 last season. He had never topped .556 in a season. His free-throw rate has climbed from .237 to .349, and his 10-to-16-foot share has collapsed from .075 to .018. He's averaging 21.5 points, below last season's 24.2, yet he's still up five spots in his draft position. A more interesting shot profile doesn't automatically mean more points. Playing alongside a player like Julius Randle likely also affects how a player navigates getting shots up.

These two are still scoring, but their rank has dropped since they were drafted. A new shot mix doesn't always show up the same way in the box score.

Durant is a free-throw story. His FTr has risen from .339 to .582 (92 attempts in 12 games, 7.7 a game), above his previous career best of .523 in 2012-13 and his .392 career mark. His 3-point volume has fallen from 5.8 a game to 3.8, and he's turning it over 3.3 times a game. Durant is at 20.9 points on 49% shooting. Having Fred VanVleet back and running more offense through Alperen Şengün is putting less dependency on Durant – which is probably a good thing in the long run. Fantasy-wise, Jabari Smith Jr. and Şengün's growth has Durant adjusting his shot profile, which could lessen his value as a scorer.

Gilgeous-Alexander's rim share has fallen from 22.8% to 13.1% and his 10-to-16-foot share has risen from .273 to .303. His 3s are up to .292 on 6.7 attempts a game from .226 on 4.4 last season. SGA's free-throw rate has dropped from .465 to .339. He's still scoring 29.7 a night, so this is a small slide from a very high perch.

That's an observable redistribution that likely comes down to how much attention he's drawing without co-stars Jalen Williams and Chet Holmgren to balance out the Thunder's offensive attack. SGA is having to settle for more contested shots and rely more on his jumper. SGA has proven to be one of the most efficient guards in the league, but fantasy managers may have to accept that this could be a down season. He's shooting 45.6% from the field as of Wednesday.

This is where the shot shift is costing the most fantasy value. A big jump in one kind of shot usually takes a few weeks to settle and the efficiency tends to lag the volume. Whether that's what's happening here, or whether the new role simply isn't a good fit, is something we'll know more as we move further into the season.

In 10 games with Orlando, 100 of his 148 attempts have been 3s: 67.6%. His 3-point share had slid from .543 as a rookie to .354 last season, against a .441 career mark. His two-point attempts have fallen from 9.5 a game to 4.8 this season. His average shot distance is 23.4 feet, up from 13.5.

The results haven't been good: 39.2% from the field and 18.0 points a game, down from 20.1. The key question is whether Orlando keeps asking him to operate on such a narrow diet. If it does, last season's scoring profile may be the wrong baseline even after his shooting recovers.

Markkanen has the second-biggest 3-point jump on this list, making up .646 of his attempts, up from .402 last season. His previous career high was .570 in 2024-25. His rim share has halved, from .210 to .106, and his average shot distance has gone from 13.9 feet to 18.9. And now, he'll miss at least a month with two non-displaced fractures in his shooting hand.

He's scoring 18.0 points a game on 39% shooting, down from 26.7 in 42 games last season. A bigger share of 3s didn't produce more points here, at least so far.

Buzelis is a different case from the others in this group, because his shot mix barely changed. His 3s are .520 of his attempts, almost identical to last year's .514. What changed is how many shots he's taking. He's down from 12.5 field-goal attempts a game to about 9.8, with both his 3s (6.4 to about 5.1) and his twos (6.1 to about 4.7) falling, and his minutes slipped from 29.2 to 26.3.

His free-throw rate jumped from .247 to .429, but that's partly a smaller denominator. He's getting to the line about 4.2 times a game, up from 3.1, on far fewer shots. The extra trips haven't made up for the lost volume. He's shooting 43% with 2.8 turnovers, and he's ranked 226th. A higher free-throw rate doesn't help much when the shots aren't there to begin with. Caleb Wilson has a fractured hand and is reportedly expected to miss 4-6 weeks, so expect Buzelis to see a short-term boost in his volume.

Shot diets settle faster than shooting percentages, and that's the lens for this group. Bane and Markkanen have the clearest case: 3s are 68% and 65% of their shots, and that's likely to stick, while their 39% shooting is the part more likely to improve. More 3s  also means a lower field-goal percentage but more 3PM, so expect a trade-off in 9-cat rather than a clear gain.

Fox and Durant are getting to the line at career-high rates, which gives them some cushion on off nights, though it hasn't lifted either one's rank. Buzelis is the caution. His free-throw rate is up, but his shot volume is down, so the better rate hasn't helped his fantasy value.

Bet on the shot profile, then check whether the production is catching up.

#2835. Stars hold players-only meeting, try to diagnose issues in blowout loss against Jets

Typically after a loss, the Stars are quick to rush out of the locker room and get on with their nights.

But after Thursday's 8-3 blowout loss to the Winnipeg Jets at home, the Stars stayed put.

The coaches had left, but the locker room doors remained closed for at least 10 minutes, as the players had a meeting of their own to try to diagnose what went wrong in their worst loss of the season — and the first time in a decade where they gave up eight goals.

"It was just something that needed to happen for our group," captain Jamie Benn said of the postgame meeting.

Newcomer David Perron is a veteran in the league, now playing his 20th season in the NHL after signing with the Stars a few weeks ago. He's used to taking the lead in players-only meetings when they happen. But on Thursday, he took a backseat and learned about how his new team handles those situations.

"We have high expectations in the room," Perron said. "When you go through a tough patch, teams react, and that's what we did. We become closer as a group when we are able to talk about the things that we need to talk about."

The message from the Stars after their worst defeat of the season was that they need to respond. The Stars have never had a problem doing so, rarely going on lengthy skids and often finding bounce-back games within a few days.

But Stars coach Glen Gulutzan said the way the team responds to adversity needs to change.

"The fix is a mindset," Gulutzan said. "It was one of the better first periods that we had. ... After the first goal in the second period, we have a mindset of, OK, now we've got to get it back right away, and we will do whatever we can to get it back. That's the wrong mindset. The right mindset is just to stay with your game and keep building it back up."

The Stars did overcompensate in that way on Thursday. Rather than sticking to what was working in first period, they got away from it in the second, allowing Winnipeg to score five goals in the 20-minute period and run away with the game.

For a team full of veterans that so badly want to win, channeling that energy can be a challenge. But Gulutzan says resolving that issue may be the only way the Stars can become the resilient team again they've been known to be in the past.

Dallas Stars left wing Jason Robertson (21) moves the puck down the ice against Winnipeg Jets center Dawson Mercer (19) during an NHL hockey game at American Airlines Center, Thursday, Nov. 12, 2026 in Dallas. (Chris Swann/The Dallas Morning News)

Part of that resilience also relies on Stars goalie Jake Oettinger.

"We never got any saves either," Gulutzan said. "You combine that mentality with that, and it's a bad recipe against a team that's feeling pretty good about their game."

Oettinger was benched a minute into the third period Friday after allowing a season-high sixth goal of the game. He finished with 21 saves on 27 shots.

The Stars goalie has seen a dramatic shift in his numbers in his last six starts. After posting a .939 save percentage in his first eight starts of the season, that figure has dropped to .842 in his last six.

Oettinger said he's more concerned with his 4-2-0 record in that span than his numbers and doesn't think anything has changed for him.

Still, he said he needs to elevate his game along with his teammates.

"For me, it's how do you make a big save after you let in a goal?" he said. "We didn't do that as a team, and I didn't do that in net."

The Stars have struggled to find consistency this season. Outside of one five-game win streak that included three overtime wins, their results have been unpredictable every night.

Injuries have contributed to that, as the Stars are now down to five healthy defenseman after Lian Bichsel went down with an injury Thursday, joining already injured Miro Heiskanen and Kyle Capobianco.

But the Stars have overcome injuries to have successful seasons in the past. They're just hoping they can look better when they take the ice again this weekend for a back-to-back at home.

After a game like Thursday's, there's nowhere to go but up.

#2834. Rams place WR Puka Nacua on IR with knee, turf toe injuries

The Los Angeles Rams placed Puka Nacua on injured reserve, the team announced Thursday. The move will sideline the receiver for at least four games after he suffered multiple injuries Sunday.

In a connected move, the Rams have signed receiver Brennan Presley to the active roster from the practice squad, and signed WR Tyler Scott to the practice squad.

Nacua sustained injuries late in the Rams' win over the Washington Commanders on Sunday. He was later diagnosed with MCL and PCL sprains in his right knee and a turf toe injury.

Head coach Sean McVay told reporters Wednesday that Nacua will miss "a significant period of time." McVay initially said the receiver would not go on IR due to "some semantics with the roster," but that appears to have changed.

By being on IR, the earliest Nacua can return is against the Dallas Cowboys on Dec. 20.

Nacua, who missed two games early in the season with a hip injury, has had a strong 2026 season. In seven games, he has 70 receptions for 715 yards and four touchdowns. The Commanders' game was his most productive of the season before the injuries, as Nacua picked up 14 catches for 150 yards and a touchdown.

Nacua joins Davante Adams on IR; Adams has been there since Week 6 with a ruptured hamstring. McVay said Wednesday he is hoping for Adams to return in Week 13, meaning it will still be a couple of weeks of playing with a shrunken receiver core.

#2833. IRS announces 2027 401(k) contribution limits, raises savings cap: What it means for your money

The IRS has announced new retirement account contribution limits for 2027.

In its release on Thursday, the agency increased the 401(k) employee deferral limit to $25,500 next year, up from $24,500 in 2026. The change applies to 401(k)s, 403(b)s and most 457 plans, along with the federal Thrift Savings Plan.

The IRS also unveiled 2027 catch-up contribution limits for savers age 50 and older, new individual retirement account savings limits and higher income thresholds for Roth IRA contributions.

Starting in 2027, the 401(k) catch-up contribution limit will rise to $8,500 for savers 50 and older, up from $8,000 in 2026. But investors aged 60 to 63 can instead save an extra $11,750, based on changes enacted via Secure 2.0. That figure is up $500 from 2026.

Both amounts are in addition to the $25,500 deferral limit for 2027.

The 2027 IRA contribution limit is $7,500, unchanged from 2026, the IRS announced. IRA catch-up contributions for investors age 50 and older will be $1,100, the same as 2026.

The announcement comes one week after the agency released dozens of other inflation adjustments for 2027, including federal income tax brackets, higher capital gains brackets, estate and gift tax exemptions and provisions impacting families, among others.

In 2025, only 14% of participants maxed out their 401(k)s, according to Vanguard's 2026 How America Saves report, which was based on data from more than 1,300 qualified plans and nearly 5 million participants. The average combined savings rate, including employer deposits, was an estimated 12.1%, according to the same report.

"Before worrying about contribution limits, make sure you're capturing your full employer match," said Sean Michael Pearson, a certified financial planner with Ameriprise Financial Services in Conshohocken, Pennsylvania.

During the second quarter of 2026, about 19% of employees weren't deferring enough to 401(k)s to receive their company's full matching contribution, according to a Fidelity analysis of 27,300 corporate plans and nearly 26 million participants.

Vanguard suggests saving 12% to 15% of your pay per year, including employer contributions, depending on your income. But only one-quarter of 401(k) participants deferred more than 10% of earnings in 2025, according to Vanguard's report.

If you plan to max out your 401(k) for 2027, you should change your savings rate in December so it's active by your first January paycheck, experts say. The update could take one or two pay periods to go into effect.

The "most important habit" is consistently increasing your 401(k) savings rate over time, according to Pearson.

For example, if you bump 401(k) contributions by 1% every year, "you may barely notice the difference in your paycheck, but the long-term impact can be substantial," he said.

In 2025, about 27% of 401(k) plans automatically increased employee deferrals over time, known as "auto-escalation," according to the Plan Sponsor Council of America's yearly survey of 401(k)s. The nonprofit trade organization's survey included 753 plans.

The vast majority of plans with auto-escalation, 69%, boosted deferrals by 1% per year, the Plan Sponsor Council of America found.
